x WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
The West Valley Demonstration Project (WVDP) is located on the site of the former and only commercial spent nuclear fuel
reprocessing facility to have operated in the United States. This former reprocessing facility was built in 1963 and was operated
by Nuclear Fuel Services, Inc. (NFS) from 1966 to 1972. Since 1982, the site has been managed by the United States (U.S.)
Department of Energy (DOE) under the direction of the DOE’s Office of Environmental Management (DOE-EM). Although
the spatial size of the WVDP is not large (152 acres [0.6 km2]) compared with other nuclear cleanup sites in the U.S., the
WVDP operates under the same rigorous level of DOE oversight as the larger sites, with site-specific procedural and regulatory
requirements that align with its historical background. The WVDP is located within the 3,338-acre [13.5 km2] New York State-
owned Western New York Nuclear Service Center (WNYNSC).

The Annual Site Environmental Report (ASER) for the WVDP is prepared in accordance with DOE Order 231.1B Chg 1,
“Environment, Safety, and Health Reporting.” This report describes the environmental monitoring activities performed at the
WVDP in the calendar year. It provides information and data to members of the public, regulators, WVDP workers, and other
interested stakeholders. The DOE conducts WVDP activities in cooperation with the New York State (NYS) Energy Research and
Development Authority (NYSERDA).

Project Background
In 1980, Congress passed Public Law 96-368 (the WVDP Act) which required the Secretary of Energy to carry out a high-level
radioactive waste management demonstration project, in accordance with certain requirements. The WVDP Act is included in
its entirety in Appendix H.

In April 2010, the DOE released a Record of Decision (ROD) for the Final Environmental Impact Statement (Final EIS or FEIS)
for the WVDP and the WNYNSC (“Final Environmental Impact Statement for Decommissioning and/or Long-Term Stewardship
at the West Valley Demonstration Project and Western New York Nuclear Service Center,” DOE/EIS-0226, issued on January
29, 2010). In the FEIS, the DOE and NYSERDA evaluated four alternatives: Site-wide Removal, Site-wide Close-In-Place, Phased
Decisionmaking, and No Action. Phased Decisionmaking was selected as the preferred alternative. Under this alternative,
decommissioning is being conducted in two phases, Phase 1 and Phase 2.

The FEIS and other documents related to the WVDP activities are found at https://www.energy.gov/wvdp/document-index.

Phase 1 Site Decommissioning is currently ongoing and involves the removal of a number of contaminated facilities, soil reme-
diation, and soil and facility characterization. In addition, as part of the Phase 1 scope, Phase 1 studies were completed in 2018
to provide additional technical evaluations to facilitate future decisionmaking for the remaining site facilities.

The DOE intends to analyze alternatives for its remaining decommissioning decisions as part of a Supplement to the 2010 EIS,
or “Supplemental” EIS (SEIS), being jointly prepared with NYSERDA. Areas included within the scope of the SEIS are the Waste
Tank Farm (WTF), two former radioactive waste disposal areas, the Construction and Demolition Debris Landfill (CDDL), and the
non-source area of a radioactively contaminated groundwater plume. The SEIS will evaluate a range of alternatives including
removal, in-place closure, and a combination of those two.

Development of a Probabilistic Performance Assessment (PPA) to evaluate the decommissioning alternatives continued in 2025,
as well as continued efforts to prepare the draft SEIS.

Environmental Compliance
The WVDP continued to operate in compliance with all applicable federal, state, and county environmental laws, regulations,
permits, and standards in 2025.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 EXE-1

In 2025, there were:

 • No New York State Pollutant Discharge Elimination System (SPDES) permit effluent limit noncompliance events.

 • No exceedances of the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency’s (EPA’s) National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pol-
 lutants (NESHAP) dose standard.

 • No exceedances of the all pathway dose standard in DOE Order 458.1 Chg 4, “Radiation Protection of the Public and the
 Environment.”

In collaboration with the New York State Department of Environmental Conservation (NYSDEC), the WVDP continued to per-
form whole effluent toxicity (WET) testing on lagoon 3 discharges in 2025.

2025 Project Overview
Phase 1A Main Process Plant Building (MPPB) Deconstruction

In September 2022, the controlled deconstruction of the Main Process Plant Building (MPPB), a major project in Phase 1
decommissioning, began after years of thorough planning and deactivation activities. The deconstruction was completed in
June 2025, in 32 months, one month earlier than the planned 33-month project duration. The controlled deconstruction of the
MPPB was conducted using detailed technical planning and rigorous environmental monitoring. The deconstruction met all
radiological and non-radiological standards set by the federal, state, and local regulatory agencies.

The parts of the MPPB deconstructed in 2025 included the Process Mechanical Cell Room (PMCR), the remaining portion of the
Off Gas Cell (OGC), and the Product Purification Cell (PPC). In the last half of 2025, the WVDP focused on planning to initiate
the deconstruction of the remaining above-grade facility, including the former Fuel Receiving and Storage (FRS) building, as
well as below-grade facility deconstruction and soil removal within the MPPB footprint.

Permeable Treatment Wall (PTW) Performance

Overall, the PTW installed in November 2010 continues to mitigate the groundwater plume, reducing concentrations of
strontium-90 downgradient through 2025. Since 2021, elevated strontium-90 activity has been observed in a few PTW wells and
in some downgradient wells. In 2021, the WVDP investigated the cause of the elevated strontium-90 activity and in 2022 initiat-
ed a study to identify methods to enhance the effectiveness and extend the longevity of the PTW. The investigation confirmed
that when the facility switched from a sand/salt mixture for roadway deicing to 100% salt, the high concentrations of sodium
chloride infiltrating the groundwater from runoff negatively impacted PTW performance. As a result, the site reverted to using
a sand/salt mixture on the main parking lot and driving surfaces. Monitoring throughout 2025 indicated that minimizing the
use of salt for managing snow and ice would continue to limit negative impacts to wall performance. The site will continue to
monitor wall performance, and at this point no further additional action is planned for PTW life extension.

Tank 8D-4 Sampling and Decommissioning

Tank 8D-4 was one of the underground high-level waste (HLW) tanks built to store liquid HLW generated during spent nuclear
fuel reprocessing operations. The HLW from Tank 8D-4 was retrieved to the extent practical for vitrification, which concluded
in 2002. Confirmatory radiological characterization of remaining sludge continued in 2025. In January 2025, five samples from
Tank 8D-4 were shipped to a research institute for analysis, and in February 2025, sludge sampling field work was completed.
The data collected as part of this sampling effort will support future Phase 1B scope for heel removal and disposition. Note that
decommissioning of the HLW tanks, including 8D-4, is being evaluated as part of the SEIS.

Environmental Management System (EMS)
The third-party on-site audit in August 2025 verified that the WVDP conforms to the ISO 14001:2015 standard. The standard
specifies the requirements for establishing, implementing, maintaining, and continually improving an EMS to assist organiza-
tions in managing their environmental responsibilities efficiently and meeting environmental obligations.

EXE-2 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

Environmental Monitoring
Environmental monitoring continued on site and off site in 2025 to detect changes in the nearby environment resulting from the
current WVDP projects or historical site activities. Airborne and waterborne effluents, drinking water, and biota were sampled
in accordance with the WVDP environmental monitoring program. There were no unplanned radiological or non-radiological
releases in 2025.

Airborne Radiological Monitoring Program

During 2025, the WVDP continued to monitor radiological emissions from on-site NESHAP-approved emission points, including
three active on-site building/facility stacks and up to 15 portable ventilation units (PVUs). Radioisotope concentrations mea-
sured in the on-site stack and PVU emissions were well below the DOE’s Derived Concentration Standard (DCS) for air inhalation.

Off-site ambient air monitoring continued at the 16 ambient air sampling stations that surround the WNYNSC premises, approx-
imately one to two miles from the former MPPB. The ambient air monitors were in operation 99.08% of the time in 2025. The
ambient air monitoring results confirmed that the WVDP was compliant with the NESHAP concentration limits.

Surface Water Radiological Releases Monitoring Program

In 2025, waterborne radiological releases from the site were monitored at three on-site discharge points (two natural streams
and one controlled outfall from lagoon 3) and at five additional surface water monitoring points on on-site streams across the
WVDP premises. Off-site surface water was sampled at two downstream locations on Cattaraugus Creek and one upstream
location on Buttermilk Creek. Measured concentrations of radioisotopes in off-site creeks were orders of magnitude lower than
the DCS concentrations.

Groundwater Monitoring Program

Radiological isotopes and nonradiological chemical parameters and water levels were monitored at 64 wells and seepage loca-
tions across the WVDP site, according to the WVDP groundwater monitoring plan. Chromium and nickel were detected above
their NYS Technical and Operational Guidance Standards at two wells in 2025. The source of chromium and nickel is believed
to be corrosion of the stainless steel well screens. Arsenic was detected marginally above the Technical Standard level in a well
located in proximity to the MPPB deconstruction footprint. Among volatile/semivolatile organics, tributyl phosphate (TBP) was
detected above the Practical Quantitation Limit (PQL) at one monitoring well, however, the concentration of this chemical has
been decreasing since 1996. Elevated levels of gross beta activity, primarily composed of strontium-90, are present within the
well-defined groundwater plume on the north plateau. Monitoring of the wells, where elevated strontium-90 has been ob-
served, continued in 2025. Elevated gross alpha levels were also detected in wells directly downgradient of the former MPPB,
as a result of the deconstruction activities and the use of dust-suppression water. The sampling results of emerging contami-
nants identified by the EPA (organic chemicals such as 1,4-dioxane and 1,2,3-trichloropropane) in groundwater were below the
regulatory limits.

Drinking Water Monitoring Program

Drinking water was analyzed according to the Cattaraugus County Health Department (CCHD) requirements for radiological and
non-radiological analytes, including 1,4-dioxane. The drinking water wells were sampled for per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances
(PFAS) compounds at an EPA Method 533-certified laboratory. All results were below the regulatory limits.

Radiological Dose Assessment
Airborne and Waterborne Doses

The maximum dose to an off-site individual from airborne emissions associated with WVDP activities was estimated using mea-
sured data from the ambient air samplers. The estimated annual airborne dose in 2025 was 0 millirem (mrem) (0 millisievert
[mSv]), based on annual averages of radioisotopic concentrations, all of which were nondetects.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 EXE-3

The maximum waterborne dose to an off-site individual from WVDP activities in 2025 was estimated based on the measured
radioisotopic concentrations in surface water discharges from the site. The estimated annual waterborne dose was 0.009 mrem
(0.00009 mSv).

The total estimated maximum dose to an off-site individual, including airborne and waterborne sources in 2025, was
0.009 mrem (0.00009 mSv), which is well below the annual 100-mrem limit established by DOE Order 458.1. In comparison,
the average dose to a member of the public in the U.S. from natural background and artificial sources is 620 mrem per year
(National Council on Radiation Protection and Measurements [NCRP] Report No. 160, 2009). 0.009 mrem/year exposure from
the WVDP activities is only 0.002% compared with the radiation dose that the general public receives in a year.

Dose from Food

Radioisotope concentrations in milk and venison (white-tailed deer) samples collected near the WVDP site were indistinguish-
able from the concentrations of background (control) samples. The estimated dose from food samples collected near-site and
background was 0.43 mrem/year for an individual residing in the regions of the WVDP. It was 0.07% of the natural radiation that
a U.S. resident would receive annually.

Dose to Biota

The dose to on-site and near-site biota was evaluated based on the radioisotope concentrations in on-site surface water and
on-site and near-site soil and sediments. The results confirmed that aquatic and terrestrial biota populations (both plants and
animals) were not exposed to doses above the DOE biota dose standard of 1 rad/day (radiation absorbed dose) for aquatic
animals and terrestrial plants, and 0.1 rad/day for riparian and terrestrial animals.

(Soil, sediment, crops, and fish are sampled every five years and will be sampled next in 2027.)

Waste Management
The final RCRA closure certification of the Analytical and Process Chemistry (A&PC) Hot Cells was accepted by NYSDEC in Jan-
uary 2025. The Hazardous Waste Closure Certification report for the Lag Storage Area (LSA) #2 Hardstand was also accepted
by NYSDEC in January 2025. During the 32 months of MPPB deconstruction, 1,556 containers of low-level waste (LLW), mixed
waste, and universal waste, totaling 58 million pounds, were safely shipped to off-site facilities.

Quality Assurance (QA)
The data presented in this report are validated in accordance with WVDP QA procedures. The WVDP QA program includes
evaluations of the performance of subcontract laboratories and routine assessments of the environmental and regulatory com-
pliance programs. Subcontract laboratories that analyze WVDP environmental samples participated in independent radiological
and nonradiological constituent performance evaluation studies. In these studies, environmental test samples with concentra-
tions only known by the testing agency were analyzed by the laboratories. Of 201 performance evaluation analyses conducted
for the WVDP in 2025, 98.5% were within accepted limits.

Worker Safety
As of December 31, 2025, WVCA and its subcontractors achieved 312,436 consecutive work hours without lost-time work be-
cause of accidents or illness. There were zero hours of lost time attributed to injury or illness in calendar year (CY) 2025. The
WVCA was awarded both the DOE Voluntary Protection Program (VPP) Star of Excellence and the National Safety Council (NSC)
Occupational Excellence Achievement Award for its safe work achievement in 2025.

Conclusion
In 2025, the WVDP safely concluded Phase 1A deactivation and decommissioning work and shifted its focus to preparing the
activities for Phase 1B, which are anticipated to continue for the next 10 to 15 years. During 2025, the WVDP complied with all
environmental regulations and DOE directives. The environmental data collected and evaluated in 2025 indicate that the WVDP

EXE-6 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

 CHAPTER 1
 INTRODUCTION
Site History
The West Valley Demonstration Project (WVDP) is a U.S. Department of Energy (DOE)-operated cleanup site located within the
New York State-owned Western New York Nuclear Service Center (WNYNSC) where commercial spent nuclear fuel reprocessing
operations were previously conducted in the late 1960s and early 1970s.

The scope of the DOE’s mission at the WVDP is established by the WVDP Act of 1980 (PL 98-356) (Appendix H), signed by
then-President Jimmy Carter. Under the Act, the Secretary of the Department of Energy is required to carry out the following
activities:

  (1) Solidify, in a form suitable for transportation and disposal, the high level radioactive waste at the WNYNSC ('Center') by
 vitrification or by such other technology which the Secretary determines to be the most effective for solidification.

  (2) Develop containers suitable for the permanent disposal of the high level radioactive waste solidified at the Center.

  (3) As soon as feasible, transport, in accordance with applicable provisions of law, the waste solidified at the Center to an
 appropriate Federal repository for permanent disposal.

  (4) In accordance with applicable licensing requirements, dispose of low level radioactive waste and transuranic waste pro-
 duced by the solidification of the high level radioactive waste under the project.

  (5) Decontaminate and decommission —

 a) the tanks and other facilities of the Center in which the high level radioactive waste solidified under the project was
 stored,

 b) the facilities used in the solidification of the waste,

 c) any material and hardware used in connection with the project, and

 in accordance with such requirements as the Commission may prescribe.

Item numbers (1) and (2) above were safely completed as of the end of 2002. Item number (3) is pending the availability of a
Federal repository for permanent disposal of the solidified high-level radioactive waste. Significant progress has been and con-
tinues to be made in low-level radioactive waste disposal at off-site facilities, in support of item number (4), while transuranic
waste remains safely stored on site pending the availability of off-site disposal locations. During 2025, significant progress con-
tinued toward facility decontamination and decommissioning, item number (5), as summarized more fully below, and in future
Chapters.

Site Location and General Environmental Setting
The WVDP is located in western New York State (NYS), about 30 miles (mi) (50 kilometers [km]) south of Buffalo, New York. The
WVDP facilities currently occupy a security-fenced area of about 152 acres (61 hectares [ha]) within the 3,338-acre (1,351 ha)
WNYNSC premises in the Town of Ashford in northern Cattaraugus County (Figure 1-1). An aerial photo of the WVDP is present-
ed in Figure 1-2.

Climate. Although extremes of 99oF (37oC) and -20oF (-29oC) have been recorded in western New York (NY) (weather station:
Buffalo, NY), the climate is moderate, with an average annual temperature of 48.2oF (9oC). (Average data for 1940 to 2025 from
The National Weather Service, National Oceanic, and Atmospheric Administration [NOAA], https://www.ncdc.noaa.gov/cag).
Precipitation is markedly influenced by Lake Erie to the west and, to a lesser extent, by Lake Ontario to the north.

 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 1-3

Based on data collected at the on-site meteorological tower, the recent 10-year average annual precipitation at the WVDP
was 41.34 inches/year (105.00 cm). Total precipitation in 2025 was 37.29 inches (94.72 cm), about four inches lower than the
10-year average. Predominant wind directions are generally from the northwest and southeast that follow valleys extending to-
ward Lake Erie, located north-northwest of the WVDP. The on-site meteorological tower continuously monitors the site-specific
wind speed, wind direction, temperature, and barometric pressure. These data are accessible in real-time to site operations
personnel.

Ecology. The WNYNSC lies within the northern deciduous forest biome, and the diversity of its vegetation is typical of the re-
gion. Equally divided between forest and open land, the WNYNSC premises provide a habitat attractive to white-tailed deer
and various indigenous and migratory birds, reptiles, and small mammals. No species on the federal endangered species list are
known to reside on the WNYNSC premises.

Geology and Hydrology. The WVDP premises lie on the Allegheny Plateau in New York State (NYS) at an elevation of approx-
imately 1,300 to 1,450 feet (ft) (approximately 400 to 440 meters [m]) above mean sea level (MSL). The underlying geology
includes a sequence of glacial sediments above shale bedrock. The WVDP site is drained by three small streams (Franks Creek,
Quarry Creek, and Erdman Brook) and is divided by a stream valley (Erdman Brook) into two general areas: the north plateau
and the south plateau.

Franks Creek, which receives drainage from Erdman Brook and Quarry Creek, flows into Buttermilk Creek, which enters
Cattaraugus Creek and flows westward away from the WNYNSC premises (Figures A-1 and A-5 in Appendix A). Cattaraugus
Creek ultimately drains into Lake Erie to the northwest.

Relevant Demographics
Although several roads and a railway approach or pass through the WNYNSC, the public is prohibited from accessing the
WNYNSC. No unescorted public access is allowed on the WVDP site.

Land near the WNYNSC is used primarily for agriculture and arboriculture. Cattaraugus Creek downstream of the WNYNSC is
used locally for fishing and for recreational activities (such as swimming and kayaking). Although some water is taken from the
creek to irrigate nearby golf course greens and tree farms, no public drinking water is drawn from the creek before it flows into
Lake Erie. Water from Lake Erie is used as a public drinking water supply. Industrial water and potable water used for the WVDP
activities are supplied by bedrock groundwater wells.

The communities of West Valley, Riceville, the town of Ashford, and the Village of Springville are located within approximately
5 miles (8 km) of the WVDP. The population around the site is sparse with an average population density of Cattaraugus County
of about 58 persons/mi2 (23 persons/km2) (United States [U.S.] Census 2020). No major industries are located within this area.

Deconstruction and Decommissioning Process
In January 2010, the DOE and NYSERDA issued the “Final Environmental Impact Statement (FEIS) for Decommissioning and/
or Long-Term Stewardship at the West Valley Demonstration Project and Western New York Nuclear Service Center,” DOE/
EIS-0226. In the FEIS, the DOE and NYSERDA evaluated four alternatives: Site-wide Removal, Site-wide Close-In-Place, Phased
Decisionmaking, and No Action. Phased Decisionmaking was identified as the preferred alternative. Under this alternative, it
was decided that the decommissioning would be conducted in two phases , Phase 1 and Phase 2. For the purposes of imple-
mentation, DOE first contracted for an initial portion of Phase 1 (designated as Phase 1A) which primarily addressed decontam-
ination and removal of above-grade facilities, to include the former Main Process Plant Building (MPPB) and High Level Waste
(HLW) Vitrification Facility, along with other ancillary facilities located on the northern portion of the WVDP premises. The Phase
1A contract was completed in June of 2025. In late June of 2025, the DOE initiated a new contract for implementation of Phase
1B scope. Therefore, 2025 marked the year of completion of the above-grade removal of many former site facilities and the
initiation of planning to support below-grade structure and soil removal remedial actions.

In parallel with Phase 1 decommissioning actions, the DOE and NYSERDA are jointly preparing a Supplemental EIS (the SEIS)
to evaluate alternatives for implementing the next phase (Phase 2) of decommissioning. Figure 1-3 graphically represents the
decommissioning phases and the scope of facilities being addressed within each of the phases.

The SEIS is being prepared pursuant to the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) and the New York State Environmental
Quality Review Act (SEQR). The draft SEIS will be released for public comment once the probabilistic performance assessment
(PPA) modeling and technical analyses are complete. A six-month period for public comment on the SEIS will follow the issu-
ance of the draft report. A final SEIS will subsequently be issued, followed by the Phase 2 decommissioning Record of Decision
(ROD) and Findings Statement. Phase 2 decisions will be informed by Phase 1 and other scientific studies being performed at
the WVDP site.

Work on the long-term PPA for the WVDP site continued in 2025. The PPA simulates the outcomes of decommissioning and site
end-stage alternatives, which are described in the FEIS, using computer modeling. The scenarios for simulation include HLW
tank decommissioning options, erosion and natural disaster risk assessment, and a long-term health risk assessment. The sim-
ulation results will be evaluated in the SEIS.

By the late-2030s, the DOE expects to complete the decommissioning of the below-grade portions of the MPPB and the Vitri-
fication Facility (VF) and the decommissioning of the site’s radioactive water treatment system, including four active lagoons
and one closed lagoon. By the late-2030s, the DOE expects to complete soil remediation efforts in the Waste Management Area
(WMA)-1 (former MPPB footprint area) and WMA-2 (east of WMA-1). Figure 1-4 on the next page shows the status of the MPPB
in September 2022, when the MPPB deconstruction started, and the state of the WVDP site in September, 2025. Figure 1-5 on
page 1-7 shows the planned end-stage status of Phase 1B.

MPPB Deconstruction Completion
The deconstruction of above grade portion of the MPPB was completed in June of 2025, with the last remaining facility, the
Fuel Receiving and Storage (FRS), to be completed in 2026. As of the end of 2024, approximately 75% of the MPPB deconstruc-
tion had been completed. The deconstruction had been planned to be completed over a 33-month period and was safely and

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 1-5

compliantly completed in 32 months. The first half of 2025 was dedicated to deconstructing the remaining structures, some
of which were highly contaminated. The structures deconstructed in the period included the Process Mechanical Cell Room
(PMCR), the remaining portion of the Off Gas Cell (OGC), and the Product Purification Cell (PPC). The former MPPB footprint
was covered with a temporary geomembrane, as seen in Figure 1-4.

During the planning stage of deconstruction, air dispersion modeling was conducted using planning assumptions such as
radiological inventory of the structures, deconstruction methods, waste staging and packaging approaches, and predicted
wind conditions, to ensure emissions remained safe and compliant. Environmental monitoring results conducted throughout
demolition verified that the work was completed in compliance with regulatory requirements and radiological dose standards.

Radiological Waste Disposition

The WVDP utilized three primary disposal facilities for the site's radiological waste disposition in 2025: Energy Solutions in Utah,
Waste Control Specialists (WCS) in Texas, and the Nevada Nuclear Security Site (NNSS) in Nevada. The waste from the structure
deconstruction was packaged and shipped to off-site facilities.

containers and specialty waste containers used to ensure compliant packaging, transportation and disposal.

Minimal Environmental Impact

Emissions in the first quarter of 2025 (2.0E-05 mrem/quarter) represented 0.0002% of EPA's NESHAP standard, 10 mrem/year.
When all quarter results were averaged, the annual airborne effective dose equivalent (EDE) to off-site individuals was zero. The
estimated maximum annual waterborne exposure to the maximally exposed individual was 0.009 mrem/year, 0.003% of annual

natural background radiation (310 mrem/year). Population (collective) dose to the residents in a 50-mile (80-km) radius from
the WVDP activities was also minimal (0.1 person-rem, 0.1% of the 100 mrem/year DOE standard.) These monitoring results
attest that the years of thorough planning and the preparation led to safe and successful completion of the MPPB above-
ground structure deconstruction.

Continued and Initiated Projects in 2025
  • Former HLW 8D-4 Tank Sampling. The characterization of sludge remaining in the 8D-4 tank continued in 2025. In January
 2025, five samples from Tank 8D-4 were shipped to a research institute for analysis, and in February 2025, sludge sampling
 field work was completed with a final characterization report issued on the remaining sludge and liquid. Tank 8D-4 charac-
 terization activities show a sludge volume of 920 gallons with 1,840 gallons of liquid as of December 31, 2025. The data will
 be utilized to support future disposition planning for the sludge and liquid heel remaining in Tank 8D-4.
  • Lake 1 Spillway construction. Two lakes and dams are located on the WNYNSC property, about three miles south of the
 MPPB area, and are maintained to provide water for fire protection and State Pollutant Discharge Elimination System
 (SPDES) discharge flow augmentation for the WVDP. The WVDP rail spur and an access roadway are located parallel to the
 lakes and run along the crest of both dams. Lake overflow spills into Buttermilk Creek, flowing from southeast to the east
 side of the WNYNSC property. The dams and spillway area have suffered progressive erosion in recent years. Following an
 independent engineering review of the lakes and dams, the DOE and the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers (USACE) began the
 construction of a concrete spillway in 2025. The spillway construction, reconstruction of culverts on Buttermilk Creek, dam
 removal, and flow rerouting projects will continue into 2026.
  • Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA) Activities. An updated Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA)
 Hazardous Waste Part A (interim status) application was submitted to the New York State Department of Conservation
 (NYSDEC )in June 2025. In 2025, the WVDP began the review and characterization of the wastes that may be generated in
 the next Phase 1B and Phase 2 decommissioning and possible disposition pathways of those wastes.
  • Subsurface characterization. The WVDP and its contractor initiated subsurface characterization efforts in September 2025
 to support engineering design efforts for the future WMA-1 below grade structure and soil removal efforts. 28 borehole
 samples were collected and analyzed for 26 radioisotopes of interest. Characterization of the soil is part of the preparation
 work before the demolition of near- and below-grade components of the MPPB. The WVDP will use this information to plan
 and design the soil remediation work scope in the former MPPB footprint. Additional borehole sampling for radiological
 and geochemical parameters began in October, 2025 and will continue into 2026.

Public Outreach
Prior to and during the MPPB deconstruction, West Valley Cleanup Alliance, LLC (WVCA) (and the former contractor, CH2M
HILL BWXT West Valley, LLC [CHBWV]) routinely informed the public and local stakeholders of the deconstruction plan and the
status of the project progress at public quarterly meetings, citizen task force meetings, and via local media. Quarterly ambient
airborne dose and surface water isotope concentrations downstream were also released publicly during the deconstruction.
This public information sharing, combined with the data in this annual report, assured the community that the WVDP was
conducting Phase 1A projects in a safe and transparent manner. Proactive communication with the public was one of the key
factors that enabled WVDP to successfully complete the major milestone on schedule.

Projects Expected In 2026
The first half of 2026 is expected to be dedicated to continued subsurface radiological and geochemical characterization and
identification and planning of the necessary infrastructure to accommodate those activities. Major infrastructure work in the
next three years includes, but is not limited to, the removal of facility-based concrete pads, groundwater and surface water
dewatering systems, on-site roads and railway system upgrades, and underground utility system rerouting.

 CHAPTER 2
 ENVIRONMENTAL COMPLIANCE STATUS
Compliance Status Overview
This chapter describes the laws, regulations, permits, and agreements pertaining to the WVDP activities, environmental
protection, and the public and workers, and the WVDP's compliance status in 2025 with regard to

 • Federal environmental protection statutes and associated state and local environmental regulations;

 • Permits required under specific environmental protection regulations; and

 • EPA or NYSDEC issuance of notices of violation or noncompliance.

The environmental permits, approvals, and registrations applicable to the WVDP are listed in Table 2-9, and the applicable
federal and state regulations are listed in Table 2-10 at the end of this chapter. Environmental monitoring results are presented
in Chapter 4, "Environmental Monitoring."

2025 Permits and License Updates
The DOE-EM awarded the WVDP Phase 1B Deactivation and Demolition (D&D) Contract to West Valley Cleanup Alliance, LLC
(WVCA) in 2025. The transition of decommissioning work from CH2M HILL BWXT West Valley, LLC (CHBWV) to WVCA began on
June 24, 2025. Most permits and licenses that existed prior to the contract change were carried over to the next phase contract.

Updates in 2025:

 • Termination of the Stormwater Pollution Prevention Plans (SWPPP) for the MPPB deconstruction following the
 completion of the project.

 • No changes were made to the SPDES permit.

 • Asbestos work continued to be performed on the MPPB deconstruction. The asbestos notification submitted in 2024
 covered the MPPB deconstruction work through the end of the deconstruction period.

Air Emissions Compliance Obligations
The EPA, NYSDEC, and the DOE have established standards for radiological and nonradiological effluents to protect human
health and the environment. The DOE applies to the EPA for approval to release limited amounts of radiological constituents
into the air and applies to the NYSDEC for permits to release limited amounts of non-radiological constituents into the air and
water. The permits issued by these agencies describe release points, management and reporting requirements, and discharge
limits on pollutants and define the sampling and analysis regimen.

Radiological Air Emissions Compliance Obligations
The Clean Air Act (CAA) and National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP)

The CAA, administered by the EPA, requires compliance with the NESHAP under Title 40 of the Code of Federal Regulations
(CFR) Part 61, Subpart H. Sources of radioactive discharges to the atmosphere are regulated directly by the EPA.

The NESHAP standard states that no member of the public may receive an Effective Dose Equivalent (EDE) greater than 10 mrem
(0.1 millisievert [mSv]) per year resulting from radionuclide emissions to the atmosphere. The EPA approval must be obtained

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 2-1

before conducting any work where it is expected that the potential to emit in dose will be 1% (0.1 mrem/year) or more of the
10 mrem standard.

Open-Air Deconstruction Using an EPA-Approved Alternative Method

NESHAP regulations allow two methods for demonstrating compliance, (1) the “measure and model” approach which involves
measuring radiological emissions in air released from point sources (such as stack effluents) and using EPA-approved com-
puter models to estimate dose to the Maximally Exposed Off-site Individual (MEOSI), or (2) the “environmental measurement”
approach which involves measuring environmental concentrations (ambient monitoring) of airborne radionuclides at ambient
air monitoring locations and evaluating dose at the critical receptor, a hypothetical off-site individual received the maximum
dose.

Prior to 2014, the WVDP used Method (1) to monitor potential airborne radiological releases. However, as above-ground struc-
ture decommissioning and internal facility removal progressed, it was anticipated that airborne emission sources would shift
from point-source stack emissions to diffuse emissions associated with MPPB deconstruction activities. For monitoring diffuse
airborne releases, Method (2) is technically more appropriate. In the fall of 2012, in consultation with the EPA, an ambient
air-monitoring network (AAMN) was installed near the WNYNSC property boundary. The network consisted of 16 low-volume
sampling stations (one for each compass sector) and one high-volume sampler (capable of measuring lower concentrations)
located in the predominant wind-direction sector. The comparability of the two environmental measurement methods was
evaluated the following year, and the results were reviewed by the EPA. With EPA approval, NESHAP compliance at the WVDP
has been demonstrated using the environmental measurement approach since 2014.

NESHAP compliance at the WVDP is demonstrated using data collected from the ambient air samplers throughout the
year. Annual average isotopic concentrations on the ambient air sampling filters are compared with the 40 CFR 61,
Subpart H, Appendix E concentration values for each radioisotope, which correspond to an annual dose of 10 mrem,
using the sum-of-fractions method. (See Chapter 5, “Dose Assessment" for additional details on the WVDP dose-esti-
mation methodology.) The WVDP monitors the operational status of the samplers in accordance with the NESHAP per-
mit, and in 2025 the annual operating percentage was 99.98%, well above the EPA’s 90% operability requirement.

Routine ambient air network sampling results are discussed in Chapter 4, "Environmental Monitoring" and are tabulated in
Appendix C. These data are used to estimate the annual dose from air emissions for NESHAP compliance.

Non-Radiological Air Emissions Compliance Obligations
The WVDP currently has an Air Facility Registration Certificate for nonradiological emission sources as required by NYSDEC and
per 6NYCRR Chapter III, Part 201. The WVDP registers to use MDI (methylene diphenyl diisocyanate) in spray foam. All potential
nonradiological point sources of air emissions are evaluated annually against permitting requirements, and the results in 2025
were determined to be exempt or below permitting release limits.

Asbestos releases are regulated separately from other nonradiological releases under NESHAP and New York State Department
of Labor (NYSDOL) regulations (12 NYCRR Part 56). The asbestos NESHAP and NYSDOL regulations require notifications to the
EPA and NYS, respectively, before deconstruction of any structure that could contain asbestos or asbestos-containing material
(ACM) in an amount exceeding regulatory thresholds to ensure the safety of the workers and the public. Table 2-6 provides a

Air Quality Compliance Update for 2025

All detected airborne releases of radiological constituents during the MPPB deconstruction and subsequent facility removal
activities in 2025 were within permissible EPA limits and DOE guidance. The estimated dose to an off-site resident from WVDP
activities in 2025 was zero mrem/year when isotopic concentrations were averaged over the entire calendar year. There were
no releases that were out of compliance with EPA and NYSDEC regulations as summarized in Table 2-1.

Surface Water Compliance Obligations
Surface Water Radiological Release Compliance Obligations

Releases of radiological constituents in water effluents are subject to the requirements in DOE Order 458.1, “Radiation Protection
of the Public and the Environment,” and DOE-STD-1196-2022, “Derived Concentration Technical Standard.” DOE Order 458.1
requires environmental monitoring of the air, water, groundwater and biota in order to ensure that the maximum potential
public radiation dose from all pathways remains under 100 mrem/year. DOE-STD-1196-2022 establishes Derived Concentration
Standards (DCSs) to be used in the design and conduct of radiological environmental programs at DOE facilities. (See the inset
box, "Radiological Data Evaluation" in Chapter 4, "Environmental Monitoring," for additional information on DCSs.)

Radiological Surface Water Release Update for 2025
All waterborne releases of radiological constituents from the WVDP in 2025 (from the SPDES outfall 001 [location code WNSP001]
and natural surface water effluents from two discharge points) were within permissible limits. The total estimated dose from
the waterborne releases via all pathways was well below the DOE 100 mrem/year limit.

Surface Water Non-Radiological Release Compliance Obligations
Clean Water Act (CWA) and SPEDES Permit

The Clean Water Act (CWA), administered in NYS by NYSDEC under EPA-delegated authority, requires that all process and storm-
water discharges from the site comply with the WVDP SPDES permit. The current site permit was issued in 2011 and regulates
nonradiological liquid discharges through the site’s monitored wastewater treatment system outfall and stormwater outfalls.
The SPDES Permit is issued in compliance with Title 8 of Article 17 of the NYS Environmental Conservation Law (ECL), with the
CWA. With NYSDEC's approval, the WVDP is currently continuing to operate under the 2011 SPDES permit.

Water Withdrawal and Drinking Water Compliance Obligations
New York State is one of the participating states in the Great Lakes - St. Lawrence River Basin Water Resources Compact Council
and regulates water withdrawal systems with a withdrawal capacity of 100,000 gallons per day (gpd) or more. NYSDEC manages
this water withdrawal and reporting program under the New York State Environmental Conservation Law (NYS ECL) Article 15,
Title 5, and NYCRR 601.5. The WVDP reports the amount of groundwater and reservoir water withdrawn annually to NYSDEC
pursuant to the site water withdrawal permit issued in December 2019.

At the WVDP, potable and industrial water has been supplied by two groundwater wells located on the site since 2014. Prior to
this, water was supplied by two reservoirs immediately south of the site. Lakes 1, one of two lakes, also called reservoirs, cur-
rently provide water through an underground pipe that extends from the reservoirs to the north plateau for fire protection and
SPDES discharge flow augmentation. The CCHD has assigned the WVDP drinking water (potable water) system a NYS tracking
number and determines the required sampling for the system annually. The CCHD requested the WVDP develop the Source
Water Protection Plan to ensure that there is no impact on drinking water from the site activities in 2014, and it is reviewed
annually.

In November 2024, the WVDP conducted a lead service line survey on the potable (drinking) water system at the site in accor-
dance with the EPA requirements under 40 CFR Part 141 Subpart I [141.48(a)] and NYSDOH requirements under 10 NYCRR
5-1.42. Written records and field verification determined that there were no lead service lines in the potable water system at
the WVDP.

Analytical results of well water and potable water at the site are presented in Appendix B-4.

Water Withdrawal Update for 2025
The 2025 WVDP water withdrawal report was submitted to NYSDEC in March 2025. In 2025, controlled discharges from lagoon
3 were performed in April, June, and November, with a total volume of 5,229,170 gallons discharged. The maximum daily
withdrawal volume in 2025 was 264,841 gallons during the April discharge. The average daily withdrawal rate in 2025 was
225,923 gallons.

The total amount of augmentation water used during these discharges totaled 9,868,500 gallons (37,356,336 liters).
Augmentation water is used during lagoon 3 discharges to reduce total dissolved solids (TDS) concentrations, which are reported
in the WVDP Discharge Monitoring Reports (DMRs).

Wildlife, Floodplain, Erosion Protection
The Endangered Species Act and the National Historic Preservation Act

Several ecological surveys of the WNYNSC premises have been conducted over the years. Except for occasional transient in-
dividuals, no plant or animal species protected under the Endangered Species Act of 1973 resides at the WNYNSC premises.
Surveys of the historic and archaeological sites revealed Native American and historic homestead artifacts on the WNYNSC
premises and in the nearby areas. None of those sites were impacted by the WVDP activities in 2025.

2-4 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

Invasive Species Control

Federal agencies are required to prevent the introduction, establishment, and spread of invasive species, as well as to erad-
icate and control populations of invasive species according to Executive Order (EO) 13751 Safeguarding the Nation from the
Impacts of Invasive Species. In 2023, wild parsnip, which can cause burns, was identified along the fence by the south parking
lot and removed. Additionally, less harmful invasive species were seen on site, including multiflora rose, phragmites, nonnative
honeysuckle, European starlings, Japanese knotweed, and house sparrows. In 2025, a spotted lantern fly was found on site and
reported to the NYSDEC. The fly was found when the site staff inspected wooden boxes delivered from another state. The WVDP
continued to monitor these invasive species on site after the finding.

Migratory Bird Treaty Act

The Migratory Bird Treaty Act (MBTA) of 1918 provides for the protection of migratory birds, their nests, and their eggs. The DOE
maintains a Bird Depredation permit that allows for the removal of bird nests from areas where they would be impacted by site
operations. Under the Bird Depredation permit, an additional registration is required for the removal or destruction of Canada
goose nests and/or goose eggs. Table 2-3 summarizes the migratory bird activities conducted during 2025.

Floodplain Management

EO 11988, Floodplain Management, was issued to avoid adverse impacts associated with the occupancy and modification of
floodplains. The WVDP is required to report when it develops new floodplains or conducts activities that would impact the ex-
isting 100-year floodplain within the premises. No such development or activities were performed in 2025.

Lake 1 Spillway Update

Two lakes (1 [south side] and 2 [north side]) are located about 0.3 miles south of the former MPPB area. A canal connects the
lakes, and each lake has a dam on the east side. The lake 2 dam discharges to Buttermilk Creek flowing on the east side of the
site. A spillway extending southeast from lake 1 discharges overflow from the lakes to Buttermilk Creek (Figure A-4). This sys-
tem was conceived and designed in the late 1950s through the early 1960s and provides water for fire protection and SPDES
discharge flow augmentation for the WVDP. The WVDP rail spur and lake access roadway are located parallel to the lakes and
run along the crest of both dams.

In recent years, the dams and the spillway have suffered progressive erosion. An independent engineering review of the lakes
and dams was performed in 2021 by McMahon and Mann Consulting Engineers, Inc. In 2023, a meeting with NYSDEC per-
sonnel was held, and NYSDEC inspected the dams, canal, and spillway. The DOE, together with the United States Army Corps
of Engineers (USACE,) evaluated the NYSDEC's inspection results and made engineering recommendations for repairs and
improvements. A final design of the spillway reinforcement was proposed by the USACE in 2024. The construction of the lake
1 spillway improvement began in 2025 and completed in October 2025. See the construction progress photos on Figure 2-1.
The railway continued to be used in 2025 to transport waste off site. Routine maintenance and inspections of the railway also
continued to be performed in 2025.

Environmental Protection Obligations
National Environmental Policy Act of 1969 (NEPA) and EIS
The categories of the NEPA documentation include categorical exclusion (CX), environmental assessment (EA), and EIS. CXs
describe actions that will not have a significant effect on the environment. EAs are used to evaluate the extent to which a pro-
posed action, not categorically excluded, will affect the environment. Based on the analyses presented in an EA and considering
regulatory agency, stakeholder, and public comments, the DOE may determine that a proposed action is not a major federal
action significantly affecting the quality of the human environment within the meaning of NEPA. Consequently, the DOE may
issue a notice indicating the finding of no significant impact (FONSI) and therefore would not require the preparation of an EIS.

If a proposed action has the potential for significant environ-
mental effects, an EIS would be prepared that describes pro-
posed alternatives to the action and explains the effects of
each. Based on the analyses presented and considering regula-
tory agency and public input, the DOE will determine the pre-
ferred alternative and issue a ROD regarding the action.

Final Environmental Impact Statement (FEIS).

In January 2010, the DOE and NYSERDA issued the “Final En-
vironmental Impact Statement for Decommissioning and/
or Long-Term Stewardship at the West Valley Demonstration
Project and Western New York Nuclear Service Center,” DOE/
EIS-0226. In the FEIS, the DOE and NYSERDA evaluated four al-
ternatives: Site-wide Removal, Site-wide Close-In-Place, Phased
Decisionmaking, and No Action. Phased Decisionmaking was
identified as the preferred alternative. Under this alternative,
decommissioning will be conducted in two phases as shown in
Figure 1-3. Decommissioning the MPPB was part of the Phase 1
EIS work.

Record of Decision (ROD). On April 14, 2010, the DOE issued the ROD for the FEIS, selecting the phased decisionmaking alter-
native. During Phase 1 Site Decommissioning, a number of highly contaminated facilities are being removed under a facilities
disposition contract awarded in 2011. The original estimated cost for all of the Phase 1 work was approximately $1.2 billion
(FEIS, 2010). Phase 1 was estimated to take up to 10 years, during which time the DOE will manage the site’s remaining facilities
in a safe manner. Phase 1B contract awarded in 2025 has a 10-year ordering period with a maximum value of up to $3.0 billion
that can be performed for up to 15 years.

Phase 1 Studies. Phase 1 studies were scientific studies conducted to facilitate interagency consensus necessary for the
completion of decommissioning the remaining facilities following the completion of Phase 1. The Phase 1 studies provide
technical evaluations supporting the SEIS preparation. The final reports on these studies, published in 2018, are available at
https://wvphaseonestudies.emcbc.doe.gov/php/pas-update.html.

Supplemental EIS (SEIS). After completing an EIS (the 2010 FEIS) and issuing an ROD in 2010 for Phase 1 decisions, the DOE
and NYSERDA determined that the preparation of an SEIS would further the purposes of NEPA and SEQR. The Phase 2 decision,
which will be informed by the SEIS, will determine the decommissioning approach for the remaining facilities. The SEIS will eval-
uate a range of alternatives for the remaining facilities for which DOE/NYSERDA have decommissioning responsibility including
removal, in-place closure, and a combination of those two. SC&A, Inc. is preparing the SEIS under a DOE contract awarded in
April 2017 that was modified in 2022. The draft SEIS will be released for public comment once the PPA modeling and technical
analyses is complete.

2-6 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

Waste Management
The DOE sites that manage radioactive waste are required to develop, document, implement, and maintain a site-wide radioac-
tive waste management program under DOE Order 435.1 Chg 2, "Radioactive Waste Management." The order aims to ensure
that all radioactive waste generated at DOE sites is managed in a manner that protects workers and public health.

At the WVDP, waste is managed in accordance with:

• "Waste Acceptance Manual" for HLW,

• "TRU Waste Management Program Plan" for TRU waste, and,

• "Low-Level Waste (LLW) Management Program Plan" for LLW, and

• "Site Treatment Plan (STP)" for the radioactive component of mixed waste.

Waste Treatment, Storage, and Disposal

Low-level radioactive waste generated at the WVDP is shipped to off-site storage and disposal facilities. The DOE Consolidated
Audit Program (DOECAP), under DOE Order 414.1E, "Quality Assurance," ensures that contracted commercial treatment, stor-
age, and disposal facilities (TSDFs) are audited on their ability to provide services in a manner that is technically defensible and
legally compliant. The WVDP conducts the QA of contracted analytical laboratories and waste storage and disposal facilities
annually and submits an annual TSDF report to the DOE. The WVDP QA program is described in Chapter 7, "Quality Assurance."
The RCRA section below describes the waste management compliance and activities at the WVDP in detail.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 2-7

Comprehensive Environmental Response, Compensation, and Liability Act of 1980 (CERCLA)
The CERCLA, and the Superfund Amendments and Reauthorization Act of 1986 (SARA) established by CERCLA, provides the
regulatory framework for cleaning up, containing, or treating the releases of hazardous substances and for remediating inactive
hazardous waste disposal sites. If a hazardous substance spill exceeds a reportable quantity, CERCLA reporting requirements
are triggered.

Emergency Planning and Community Right-to-Know Act (EPCRA)

The EPCRA, also known as the Superfund Amendments and Reauthorization Act (SARA) Title III, is a federal law passed in 1986
to inform the public of potential environmental hazards posed by the storage and handling of hazardous or toxic chemicals at
facilities in their communities. The WVDP is required to maintain Safety Data Sheets (SDSs) under the EPCRA that describe the
properties and health effects of all chemicals used and stored on site. Annual reporting requirements are based on the types
and quantities of these potentially hazardous or toxic chemicals. As shown in Table 2-4, the WVDP did not use or store any
EPCRA-listed extremely hazardous substances exceeding their threshold planning quantity. The 2025 inventory of chemicals
stored at the WVDP is provided in Table 2-5 and was provided to state and local emergency response organizations and the
nearby local fire departments.
Resource Conservation and Recovery Act (RCRA)
RCRA and its implementing regulations govern the life cycle of hazardous waste from “cradle-to-grave” and mandate that gen-
erators take responsibility for ensuring the proper treatment, storage, and ultimate disposal of their wastes. A hazardous waste
permit is required for facilities that store large quantities of hazardous waste for more than 90 days or treat and/or dispose of
hazardous waste at the facility.

The EPA is responsible for issuing guidelines and regulations for the proper management of solid and hazardous waste (includ-
ing mixed [radioactive and hazardous] waste). In NYS, the EPA has delegated the authority to issue permits and enforce these
regulations to the NYSDEC. In addition, the New York State Department of Transportation (NYSDOT) is responsible for issuing
guidelines and regulations for labeling, packaging, and reporting spills of hazardous and mixed wastes while in transit.

The WVDP is a hazardous waste generator and permitted TSDF, requiring RCRA corrective actions, and routine RCRA reporting
of hazardous waste activities to NYSDEC. An agreement between the DOE, NYSERDA, EPA, and NYSDEC (the §3008[h] Consent
Order) directs the implementation of the WVDP RCRA corrective action program. (See the inset, "RCRA Permit and §3008(h)
Consent Order History at the WVDP" on the next page.)

2-8 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

 RCRA Permit and §3008(h) Consent Order History at the WVDP
 Hazardous Waste Permitting - RCRA Interim Status Permit Application. In 1984, the DOE notified the EPA of hazardous
 waste activities at the WVDP and identified DOE-WVDP as a hazardous waste generator. In 1990, to comply with Title 6 New
 York State Official Compilation of Codes, Rules, and Regulations (6 NYCRR) Part 373-3, a RCRA Part A (i.e., Interim Status
 or Part A) Permit Application for the WVDP was filed with NYSDEC for activities associated with storage and treatment of
 hazardous waste. The WVDP has operated under interim status ever since. RCRA facility operations are limited to those
 described in the Part A Permit Application and must comply with interim status regulations unless certain exemptions apply.
 The RCRA Part A Permit Application must be revised prior to changes to the WVDP’s RCRA waste management operations.

 According to Part A requirements, the DOE prepares closure plans for the hazardous waste management units at the WVDP.
 The closure plans are transmitted to NYSDEC for approval in anticipation of closure activities. A 30-day public comment
 period will follow to solicit public feedback. The plan will be revised to address the public and NYSDEC comments or changes
 in activities if necessary. To complete the closure of a RCRA unit, all wastes are removed, and other actions, such as decon-
 tamination or removal of structures, are taken to meet the specific RCRA closure requirements. A closure certification report
 is then prepared for approval by NYSDEC to document closure was performed in accordance with the NYSDEC-approved
 RCRA closure plan.

 RCRA Final Status Permit Application. In 2003, NYSDEC requested the submittal of a 6 NYCRR Part 373-2 Permit Application
 (i.e., Final Status or Part B) for the WVDP, which was transmitted to NYSDEC in December 2004. On April 16, 2009,
 NYSDEC requested the submittal of a revised Part B Permit Application for the WVDP, which was submitted to NYSDEC on
 September 30, 2010. On March 22, 2012, NYSDEC notified NYSERDA and the DOE that they had reassessed the WVDP RCRA
 regulatory program and that the processing of the September 30, 2010, Part B permit application, including revisions, would
 be deferred. As a result, the WVDP continues to operate as an interim status facility pursuant to its Part A Permit application.

 RCRA §3008(h) Consent Order (Docket No. II RCRA §3008(h) 92-0202) (abbreviated as "Consent Order"). Section §3008(h)
 of RCRA authorizes the EPA to issue an order requiring corrective action at RCRA Part A Interim Status facilities to protect
 human health and the environment from a release of hazardous waste or hazardous constituents to the environment
 from a Solid Waste Management Unit (SWMU). (See Table 2-10 on Page 2-25.) The DOE and NYSERDA entered into a
 Consent Order with the EPA and NYSDEC in March 1992. Consent Order activities performed at the WVDP to date include
 the following:
 • The RCRA Facility Investigation (RFI) evaluated potential releases of RCRA-regulated hazardous constituents from
 SWMUs. Final RFI reports were submitted in 1997, with no corrective actions required, with the exception of
 groundwater monitoring as outlined in the RFI and approved by the EPA and NYSDEC.

 • The Solid Waste Management Unit (SWMU) and Super SWMU (SSWMU) Assessment and Current Conditions
 Report. SSWMU refers to a unit where two or more SWMUs are treated as one larger waste management unit.
 These reports, initially submitted in 2004 and most recently updated in 2020, summarized the historical activities at
 each SWMU, providing environmental monitoring data and an update on activities performed since the RFI reports
 were submitted. See Table 2-7 for a complete list of WVDP RCRA SSWMUs.

 • As a result of reviewing the current conditions report in 2004, NYSDEC requested additional evaluations for
 six SWMUs (the NDA Burial Area, NDA Interceptor Trench, Demineralizer Sludge Ponds, lagoon 1, the CDDL, and the
 Low Level Waste Treatment Facility [LLWTF]) as Corrective Measures Studies (CMSs). These studies, submitted to
 NYSDEC and the EPA in 2010, identified and evaluated potential corrective measures and made recommendations
 on remedial alternatives.

 • The 1990 and 2008 Interim Measures (IMs) for the NDA were implemented, (1) to intercept and collect groundwa-
 ter within the NDA potentially contaminated with a mixture of n-dodecane and tributyl phosphate (TBP), and (2) to
 minimize water infiltration into the NDA and groundwater flow through the NDA, thereby minimizing the potential
 release of impacted groundwater until the final disposition of the NDA is determined. Liquid organic material was
 never observed. Therefore, with NYSDEC and EPA approval, in 2019, the Liquid Pretreatment System (LPS) building
 was removed, and a geomembrane cover was placed over the building footprint.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 2-9

Routine Reporting Required Under RCRA

Quarterly Status Reports, RCRA §3008(h) Consent Order (Docket No. II RCRA §3008(h) 92-0202). Under the Consent Order
signed in 1992, the DOE transmits two quarterly reports to the EPA and NYSDEC:

(1) A progress report summarizing all Consent Order activities at the WVDP for the previous quarter, which includes recent
 activities related to hazardous waste management, contacts with local community interest groups and regulatory agencies,
 and an inventory of mixed waste generated from decontamination, deactivation, and deconstruction activities during the
 reporting period.

(2) A groundwater exception report, summarizing RCRA groundwater monitoring results that exceed established trigger levels
 and an update on the performance of the NDA interceptor trench, geomembrane cover, and slurry wall.

RCRA Section 3016 Biennial Report. The RCRA 3016 statute requires all federal hazardous facilities to report the current status
of their hazardous waste activities to the EPA and NYSDEC.

RCRA Annual Hazardous Report. Hazardous wastes at the WVDP are managed in accordance with 6 NYCRR Part 373 and
reported to NYSDEC in the WVDP’s Annual Hazardous Waste Report. This report specifies the quantities of waste generated,
treated, and/or disposed of, and identifies the treatment, storage, and disposal facilities used for the waste.

Hazardous and universal waste is shipped off site to RCRA-permitted solid waste management facilities. Some of the universal
waste (i.e., lead-acid batteries and spent lamps) are reclaimed or recycled at off-site, authorized reclamation and recycling facil-
ities (Table 2-6). The annual/biennial hazardous waste reports are submitted through the EPA’s RCRA Info Industry Application
web-based system.

Hazardous Waste Reduction Plan with Annual Status Report or Biennial Update

The WVDP was not required to submit the Hazardous Waste Reduction Plan to NYSDEC in 2025 because the amount of report-
able, non-deconstruction, hazardous waste generated was less than the 25-ton threshold. (Note: Waste generated from decon-
struction, construction, or spill cleanup is exempt from hazardous waste reduction plans.) Last year, the WVDP submitted the
Reduction Plan was 2012.

Mixed Waste Management

Mixed waste (waste that is both radioactive and RCRA-hazardous) is also shipped off site. Mixed wastes that cannot be treated
or disposed of within one year are managed according to the Site Treatment Plan (STP), prepared by the WVDP under require-
ments of the Federal Facilities Compliance Act (FFCA) (an amendment to RCRA), in accordance with a Consent Order. The annu-
ally updated plan describes the development of treatment capabilities and technologies for treating mixed waste and updates
the mixed waste inventory. Currently, the only wastes covered under the WVDP STP are mixed TRU waste stored in containers
and mixed high-activity and TRU residual waste remaining in tank 8D-4, for which off-site disposal options remain unavailable
and on-site treatment is impractical.

RCRA Waste Management Activities for 2025

Per the Consent Order requirements, quarterly progress reports were submitted to the EPA and NYSDEC, documenting progress
on decontamination activities for SWMUs and waste generation activities. An Annual Hazardous Waste Report was submitted
to the EPA and NYSDEC to summarize hazardous and mixed waste generated, treated, and shipped in 2025. The waste volumes
generated at the WVDP in 2025 and a 10-year trend are depicted in Figure 2-3 on page 2-14. The number of hazardous waste
and mixed waste containers shipped off site was the highest in 2023 (43 containers), the year after the MPPB deconstruc-
tion began, and has decreased since then (19 containers in 2025). The net weight of universal waste shipped off site in 2025
increased compared with previous years, about three times that in 2024, due to the final phase clean-up of the deconstructed

2-10 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

MPPB footprint. See Table 2-6 on page 2-13 for the 2025 waste data summary. Groundwater monitoring continued during
2025 per the Consent Order requirements. This included submitting the quarterly RCRA groundwater exception reports to the
EPA and NYSDEC. The groundwater program and monitoring results at the WVDP are discussed in Chapter 6, "Groundwater
Protection Program."

As in previous years, the WVDP maintained open communications with NYSDEC and the EPA through monthly RCRA teleconfer-
ences throughout 2025. The WVDP is continuing to operate according to the 6 NYCRR Part 373-3, Part A (Interim Status) Permit
Application and the RCRA §3008(h) Administrative Order on Consent in 2025.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 2-11

Other notable activities of the RCRA in 2025 were:

NDA Interception Trench and the NDA Interim Measures (IMs). The NDA IM continued to perform as designed, helping to
contain the buried waste, while minimizing the potential for the release of chemical and radiological contaminants to the envi-
ronment. The volume of water pumped from the NDA interceptor trench has decreased noticeably since the removal of the
Liquid Pretreatment System (LPS) building and covering the footprint of the building with a new geomembrane in May 2019.
The volume pumped from the NDA trench in 2025 was 11,179 gallons (gal) (42,317 liters [L]), a 4% increase compared to the
volume pumped in 2024 (10,732 gal [40,625 L]). The volume pumped in 2025 was about 1.6% of the pre-IM volumes of over
700,000 gal/year (Figure 6-14). For the details of IMs, see the inset, "RCRA Permit and §3008(h) Consent Order History at the
WVDP," on page 2-9.

RCRA Inspection. In March 2025, representatives of the NYSDEC visited the WVDP and received waste container inspection
documentation. The inspection identified no violations.

RCRA Closure Certification. The demolition of the Analytical and Process Chemistry (A&PC) Hot Cells was completed, and the
final RCRA Hazardous Waste Closure Certification Report for A&PC was submitted to NYSDEC in 2024 and was formally accepted
in January 2025. The Hazardous Waste Closure Certification report for the Lag Storage Area (LSA) #2 Hardstand was submitted
in 2024 and accepted in January 2025.

HLW Tank. The Tank and Vault Drying System (T&VDS) reduced the residual liquid in tank 8D-4 by 1,207 gal (4,569 L) in 2025.
At the end of 2025, 2,760 gal (10,448 L) of liquid and solids remained in tank 8D-4. The 2025 residual liquid volume includes the
liquid reduction through evaporation by the T&VDS, 28 gallons (0.11 cubic meters) of 50 wt% NaOH added on May 22, 2025 for
corrosion control, and 1 gallon (0.004 cubic meters) of NaOH additional line flush water. The evaporation rate was maintained
in the range of previous years to lower the liquid level more rapidly so that the internal video inspections had a better chance
of viewing the solids depth/profile under the liquid.

The drying system has been operational for most of the year, with short outages for sludge sampling and a longer outage caused
by ventilation condensate that occluded a portion of the tank ventilation exhaust line. Characterization of sludge remaining in
the Tank 8D-4 tanks continued in 2025, and five samples from tank 8D-4 were shipped to a research institute for analysis in
January. As seen in the table Table 2-6, no liquid TRU wastes were generated in 2025 at the WVDP.

Nonradioactive, Nonhazardous, Regulated Waste Management (Non-RCRA). Nonradioactive, nonhazardous waste is also
shipped off site to solid waste management facilities. Sanitary wastewaters are shipped to the Buffalo Sewage Treatment Plant,
the Village of Gowanda Wastewater Treatment Plant, or the Village of Arcade Wastewater Treatment Plant for treatment and
disposal. This waste volume is provided in the annual water withdrawal report.

Waste Generation and Storage Activity Updates for 2025
In the calendar year 2025,

 • A total of 56,043 lbs (28 tons) of mixed and hazardous waste were stored on the WVDP site at the end of 2025
         (86,776 lbs [43.4 tons] at the end of 2024).
 • 3,869 lbs (1.93 tons) of universal waste were shipped off site for recycling or disposal.
 • 45,790 lbs (22.9 tons) of hazardous and mixed waste were shipped to designated radiological waste storage sites (off
         site) in 2025.
 • The Annual Hazardous Waste Report for 2025 waste activities was submitted to NYSDEC in January 2026.

Figure 2-3 shows the 10-year trends of waste generated at the WVDP. The top graph shows the increase in LLW and hazard-
ous and mixed LLW generation during the MPPB deconstruction. Another period of a LLW generation increase was during the
Vitrification Facility (VF) deconstruction conducted from September 2017 through September 2018. The bottom graph shows
the decreasing trend of TRU waste generation at the WVDP over the past 10 years. TRU waste generation was high during
decontamination activities and HLW vitrification, in preparation for the MPPB deconstruction. In 2024 and 2025, TRU waste
generation was zero.

Regulatory Requirements for Decommissioning
Before the commencement of decommissioning activities at nuclear sites, decommissioning plan documents that state the
scope of decommissioning and facility disposition must be submitted to the NRC. These planning documents must be consistent
with the preferred alternative in the EIS ROD and NYSERDA Findings statement.

The National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA)

As stated earlier in this chapter, NEPA requires federal agencies, including the DOE, to consider the environmental effects of
their proposed actions. Evaluations are performed to assess potential environmental effects associated with proposed project
activities. The level of evaluation and documentation depends upon whether a major federal action significantly affecting the
quality of the human environment within the meaning of NEPA. Since the WVDP began, a number of proposed site activities
have warranted environmental impact evaluations. A comprehensive summary of the NEPA documents published over the
years is included in Table 2-8. WVDP Environmental Assessment (EA) and EISs can be found at the following URL.
https://www.energy.gov/wvdp/document-index

The 2010 FEIS provides the blueprint for all activities currently underway at the WVDP. No new major NEPA documents were
initiated in 2025.

2-14 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

Phase 1 Decommissioning Plan (DP) for the WVDP

On December 5, 2008, the DOE issued the “Phase 1 Decommissioning Plan for the West Valley Demonstration Project, West
Valley, NY” (73 Federal Register [FR] 74162) and transmitted it to the NRC review. The DP was prepared to meet WVDP obligations
to the NRC as directed under the WVDP Act. The DP addressed Phase 1 of the proposed two-phased approach for WVDP decom-
missioning, consistent with the preferred alternative selected in the ROD, detailed in the "National Environmental Policy Act of
1969 (NEPA) and EIS" section, and the NYSERDA Findings Statement for the WVDP and the WNYNSC. On December 18, 2009,
the DOE submitted revision 2 of the Phase 1 DP after incorporating WVPD's responses to the NRC’s comments.

On February 25, 2010, the NRC transmitted the Technical Evaluation Report (TER) for the Phase 1 DP to DOE-WVDP, concluding
that the Phase 1 DP was consistent with the preferred alternative in the EIS prepared for the NEPA. The NRC also determined
that there is reasonable assurance that the proposed Phase 1 actions will meet the decommissioning criteria.

Phase 1 Characterization Sampling and Analysis Plan (CSAP) and Final Status Survey Plan (FSSP)

The Phase 1 DP required the preparation of two supplemental documents, the CSAP and the FSSP. These two documents
provide specific details on sampling activities to support the Phase 1 decommissioning of the WVDP. The CSAP describes the
radiological environmental data collection activities (surface and subsurface soils, sediments, and groundwater) that support
the implementation of the Phase 1 decommissioning actions within the WVDP premises. The WVDP has completed four char-
acterization surveys: underground waste tank characterization, the facility characterization, RCRA facility investigations, and the
investigation of the north plateau groundwater plume. The fifth subsurface characterization survey in the Waste Management
Area 1 (WMA-1) for Phase 1B decommissioning activities began in September 2025.

The FSSP presents the information and data necessary to support conclusions about the site's final radiological status. The
FSSP provides the technical basis and sampling protocols to demonstrate that each survey unit of the WVDP premises meets
the Phase 1 radiological cleanup goals for surface and subsurface soils identified in the Phase 1 DP. The regulatory guidance
applicable to the FSSP is the U.S. NRC Regulation (NUREG)-1575 "Multi-Agency Radiation Survey and Site Investigation Manual
(MARSSIM)," NUREG/CR-5849 Manual for Conducting Radiological Surveys in Support of License Termination, and NUREG-1757
Consolidated Decommissioning Guidance, and other relevant guidance documents. The WVDP will begin preparing the FSSP
following the completion of the CSAP, while addressing the remaining decommissioning work.

NRC Work Instruction Packages (WIP) and MPPB Deconstruction

The 1981 Memorandum of Understanding (MOU), mandated by the WVDP Act (Appendix H), established the NRC's role in
reviewing and providing consultation on activities conducted at the WNYNSC and by the DOE. The agreement applies to all
aspects of construction, operations, and demolition and decontamination (D&D) activities at the WVDP. Upon completion of
the Phase 1 DP in 2010, the NRC required the preparation of work plans for the decommissioning and deconstruction of the VF
and the MPPB. These work plans are used to define the requirements and sequencing of the deconstruction work. The decon-
struction of the MPPB was performed in compliance with the MPPB D&D plan.. Each step of the deconstruction work activities
was explicitly stated in the WIPs. The WIPs were periodically updated to add additional clarification and details involved in the
planned deconstruction process.

The NRC Region 1 conducted on-site visits in February and April 2025. The representatives conducted field observations on the
MPPB deconstruction activities and were briefed on the WVDP's radiological protection, environmental monitoring, and waste
management practices. The NRC concluded that the Phase 1A Decommissioning was completing on time and in accordance
with the approved planning.

Phase 2 Decisionmaking and SEIS Preparation

The DOE and NYSERDA established the Cooperative Agreement in 1980 to implement the WVDP Act. In 1990, the first supple-
mental agreement was signed by the DOE and NYSERDA to prepare a joint EIS. As stated in Chapter 1, "Introduction," NYSERDA

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 2-15

and the DOE continued to work on the Probabilistic Performance Assessment (PPA) modeling, which is part of the Supplemental
EIS preparation, in 2025. The modeling results will be evaluated in the SEIS and are expected to support the Phase 2 decisions.

Enforcement and Compliance History Online (ECHO)

The ECHO is the EPA's official record of the current compliance status for a given DOE site or a particular facility within the site.
The WVDP participates in the EPA's ECHO Database under the Facility Registry Service Number (FRS ID) 110000326790 (West
Valley Demonstration Project.) No non-compliance was reported in 2025. The lake and dam reconstruction mentioned above is
listed under FRS ID NYR11P502.

Other Compliance Reporting
Occurrence Report

The DOE O 232.2A Chg 1, "Occurrence Reporting and Processing of Operations Information," promotes organizational learning
consistent with the DOE’s Integrated Safety Management System (ISMS). The ISMS aims to enhance mission safety at DOE
sites and share effective practices among the sites to support their continuous improvement in safety practices and adaptation
to change. Under the order, the DOE nuclear sites are required to notify DOE personnel, including National Nuclear Security
Administration (NNSA) personnel, about events that could adversely affect the health and safety of the public or the workers,
the environment, DOE missions, or the credibility of the Department. The WVDP submitted four notifications to the Occurrence
Reporting and Processing System (ORPS) in 2025 (one occurred in 2024 and was reported in 2025). Those events pertained to
the on-site utility terrain vehicle parking procedure (rolled vehicle), snow removal procedure (cut wire), operation of a TICO yard
spotter (running into an intermodal container), and an intermodal container leak at the off-site TSDF. Subsequent fact-finding
analyses identified procedural weaknesses, and corrective measures were implemented.

Project Assessment Compliance

Project assessments are conducted through the Integrated Assessment Program (IAP) at the WVDP. This program effectively
complies with applicable DOE directives, regulations, and standards, as well as the ISMS and Environmental Management
System (EMS) requirements. Chapter 3, "Environmental Management System," describes these assessment projects at the
WVDP in detail.

 requirements to protect the public and environment against undue risk from radiation
 associated with radiological activities conducted under control of DOE pursuant to the AEA, by
 ensuring that:
 (1) operations are conducted to limit radiation exposure to members of the public pursuant to
 limits established in the Order,
 (2) radiological clearance of DOE real and personal property is controlled,
 (3) potential radiation exposures to members of the public are as low as reasonably achievable
 (ALARA),
 (4) routine and nonroutine releases are monitored and dose to the public is assessed, and
 (5) the environment is protected from the effects of radiation and radioactive material.
