2-30 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

 CHAPTER 3
  ENVIRONMENTAL MANAGEMENT SYSTEM
Environmental Management System (EMS) Overview
The WVDP is committed to implementing sound stewardship practices to protect the air, water, land, and other natural and
cultural resources that may be affected by its activities at the WVDP. Environmental management and protection from potential
pollution, including radiological and non-radiological, are incorporated into the WVDP’s work planning and execution. Actions
are taken to minimize the environmental impact of the operations, in accordance with WVDP’s environmental policy.

An EMS is a management practice that enables an organization to conduct its work in a systematic manner, minimizing the envi-
ronmental impact of its operations. The WVDP EMS was designed to meet the requirements of the International Organization
for Standardization (ISO) 14001:2015 (the Environmental Management Systems [EMS] Standard). An effective EMS ensures that
appropriate operational procedures and environmental monitoring programs are in place to minimize or eliminate any potential
environmental impact from each project. Establishing an EMS also helps to systematically address regulatory requirements,
reducing the risk of noncompliance and improving environmental performance. The WVDP EMS in 2025 focused on the MPPB
deconstruction during the first half of the year. During the second half, it shifted focus to assessing environmental regulatory
requirements that need to be addressed in the Phase 1B work scope.

Integrated safety measures to protect workers from adverse health effects and injuries is another important aspect of DOE
projects. The DOE sites require their contractors to apply the Integrated Safety Management System (ISMS) according to 48 CFR
Subpart 970.5223-1, “Integration of Environment, Safety, and Health into Work Planning and Execution” and DOE O 450.2, Chg
1 (MinChg), “Integrated Safety Management, (2011)” to their activities. However, DOE Guide (G) 450.4-1C, Integrated Safety
Management System Guide (September 2011) states that applying an environmental management system (EMS) conformant
with ISO 14001:2015, such as the EMS programs at the WVDP, ensures that the above requirements in regulations and guide-
lines mentioned are met. Worker safety and health protection at DOE sites are also governed by 10 CFR Part 851, the Worker
Safety and Health Program.

 FIGURE 3-1 Structure of ISO/DOE EMS Elements

                                                           48 CFR Subpart 970.5223-1
                                                                                                       DOE O 450.2, Chg 1 (MinChg),

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 3-1

Environmental Operating Experience
The WVCA, a contractor for the WVDP, implements the five environmental elements shown in the bottom row of Figure 3-1
on the previous page. The implementation guidelines for the elements are stated in the internal Environmental Management
Systems documentation. By assessing the applicability of environmental laws and regulations before initiating a project, the
WVCA can ensure that appropriate permits and operating practices are in place. Compliance is also maintained through routine
environmental monitoring of air, surface water, drinking water, groundwater, and ambient radiation exposure. Finally, required
regulatory reports are submitted to NYSERDA, NYSDEC, the CCHD, the EPA, and the DOE.

Environmental Policy
The WVCA strives to conduct its activities in a safe, compliant, and cost-effective manner that protects human health and the
environment. To achieve these goals, WVCA establishes an environmental policy, documents the policy, and communicates
environmental responsibilities to their workforce, which includes providing environmental training to them. WVCA also imple-
ments controls to mitigate environmental hazards. The bullets below summarize the nine commitments.

 • Identify and comply with all applicable environmental laws and regulations;
 • Use practicable means to minimize or eliminate the generation of new wastes without a path for disposition;
 • Protect the unique natural, biological and cultural resources associated with WVDP activities;
 • Conserve natural resources by reusing and recycling materials, and purchasing recycled materials, and using recyclable
 materials;
 • Establish documented environmental objectives and targets and update them as necessary;
 • Consider the input of our stakeholders when weighing alternative environmental courses of action;
 • Monitor our impact on the environment and measure our performance; and communicate these results to our employ-
 ees, subcontractors, and stakeholders;
 • Promote continual improvement to our environmental management system through self-assessment and corrective
 action; and
 • Communicate this policy to all employees and subcontractors and make it available to the public and our stakeholders.

Managers are expected to take prompt action to address environmental concerns and to have zero tolerance for noncompliance
with the policy.

Environmental Aspect Analysis
“Environmental aspect” refers to the WVDP activities, products, or services that could have impact on the environment.
Activities that have regulatory implications or those that have the potential for significant environmental impacts are identified
as “significant aspects” through a quantitative ranking process, per the ISO 14001:2015 standard. The purpose of this ranking is
to focus management attention on the most important environmental concerns associated with the WVDP’s scope of work. The
WVDP identifies the environmental aspects of site activities, including energy usage and transportation functions, which might
have a significant impact on the environment. This assessment includes emissions to air, releases to water, waste management,

 Each year all planned work activities are evaluated using a ranking system developed for the EMS that is based on potential environmental and
 regulatory impacts, community concerns, and likelihood of occurrence. Under this ranking system, aspects with an overall significance of 14 or
 greater are identified as "significant aspects."

The potential significant environmental aspects identified for 2025 are summarized in Table 3-1. The activities evaluated for
their environmental aspects in 2025 included radiological and asbestos air emissions, radiological waste generation, and toxic
discharge to surface water.

Performance Objectives
The WVDP establishes environmental objectives and targets to improve environmental performance and minimize potential
environmental impacts. These objectives and measurable targets are defined and documented for relevant functions at mul-
tiple project levels (i.e., site, project, activity, and task). Objectives and targets are developed for the most significant environ-
mental aspects and are aligned with DOE’s Energy and Water Management goals. These goals are also intended to:

 • Prevent pollution;
 • Reduce environmental hazards;
 • Protect public health and the environment;
 • Avoid waste disposal costs; and
 • Improve operational capability and overall mission efficiency.

Environmental objectives and targets are re-aligned annually to support upcoming operations and work activities. Table 3-2 lists
the WVDP’s EMS objectives, targets, and results in 2025:

EMS Program Audits
An internal audit of the EMS for the ISO 14001:2015 standard was conducted in July 2025 by the WVCA Quality and Performance
Assurance department. The audit team commended the procedural matrix that the Regulatory Strategy Group developed to
assist with the EMS procedural review, while the team suggested improvements in required competence assessment during
new-hire indoctrination and employee skill evaluations.

In addition to the internal audit, American Manufacturing Services, LLC conducted an independent third-party EMS ISO
14001:2015 conformance audit in August 2025, which identified no nonconformities, while the auditor noted 10 observations
or opportunities for improvement. Observations included document version control, container labeling, training record keep-
ing, etc. The auditor commended the performance matrix developed by WVCA as holistic, providing a good indication of the
organization’s overall performance, and noted that it supported functions needed for the protection of human health, environ-
mental protection, and operational performance and control.

Efficient Operations
Energy Use
Electricity Use. In FY 2025, the WVDP used 5,878 MWh of electricity to support site activities and 18,981 MCF (one thousand
cubic feet) of natural gas (Figure 3-2). Both energy usages increased slightly, by 4% and 2% respectively, compared with FY
2024, while completing the MPPB deconstruction work. According to the U.S. Energy Information Administration, electricity
purchased in New York State averages around 30% renewable, from hydroelectric, wind, and solar sources.

Water Use. All potable and Industrial, Landscaping, and Agricultural (ILA) water is supplied by two groundwater wells, except
for the augmentation water required during lagoon 3 discharges, which is supplied by the reservoirs. In FY 2025, approximately
16% of the total water used at the WVDP was for potable water, and 84% was used for industrial purposes (Figure 3-3). Potable
water use decreased by 4.65%, and ILA water usage decreased by 11.1% compared to FY 2024. The decrease in ILA water is
attributed to reduced water usage for dust suppression during MPPB deconstruction activities, which were completed in June
2025.

Pollution Prevention and Waste Reduction. The WVDP “Waste Minimization and Pollution Prevention Awareness Plan” requires
that waste‑minimization objectives be incorporated into the work instructions for all projects and encourages the procurement
of recycled products, the reuse of existing materials, and the implementation of energy‑conserving practices. Material recycling
and reuse are tracked under the EMS. In 2025, the WVDP recycled, reused, or donated 46.1 % of all nonhazardous municipal
solid waste generated (Figure 3‑4). This percentage had declined from 2019 through 2024; however, it increased in 2025 due
to the final phase of the MPPB deconstruction work. The weight of each material shipped for recycling is summarized in Table
3‑3. (Note: This excludes construction and deconstruction debris, which is often radiologically contaminated at the WVDP and
therefore cannot be recycled.)

Strategic Acquisition. The WVDP acquisition and procurement practices continue to ensure that 100% of all eligible electronics
are Electronic Product Environmental Assessment Tool (EPEAT) registered. EPEAT is a global environmental rating system that
helps purchasers identify high-performance, environmentally preferable computers and other electronics. In FY 2025, 100%
of awarded subcontracts met strategic acquisition requirements. And 100% of all eligible electronics were EPEAT registered.

All non-radiologically contaminated electronics were recycled in FY 2025. In FY 2025, the WVDP purchased approximately
$116,045.31 of products containing recycled or bio-based materials.

Environmental Resilience. The WVDP has worked to ensure the site is safeguarded against potential challenges by prioritizing
environmental resilience. The WVDP aims to meet regulatory requirements and protect surrounding ecosystems and commu-
nities, embodying a commitment to environmental stability and safety. Future environmental disturbances, such as soil erosion
or flooding from extreme weather events, will be modeled in the Probabilistic Performance Assessment (PPA). Alternative mea-
sures that will address predicted disturbances will be evaluated in the SEIS.

Site Security: While the DOE works to fulfill the WVDP Act and completes its associated waste management activities, the
WVDP will maintain its focus on organizational resilience through site security and emergency preparedness procedures.

Other EMS Operational Elements
Occupational Radiation Protection

WVDP EMS’ aspects include not only impacts on the environment but also impacts on personnel health and safety. 10 CFR Part
851, the Worker Safety and Health Program, for DOE sites established requirements for management responsibilities, worker
rights, hazard identification, training, record-keeping, and other related aspects. The WVDP implements these rules and require-
ments in its operational procedures and continues to review whether its procedures comply with worker protection protocols.

Training

The “WVDP Worker Safety and Health Plan” describes required safety training and explains how the WVDP complies with Part
10 CFR 851. Based on individual work requirements, employees receive specialized safety training tailored to their specific
needs. Each employee’s training requirements and training completion records are maintained in a centralized system, using
specialized training records software.

Safety Record

At the WVDP, all employees participate in human performance- and behavior-based safety training to help reduce errors and
prevent accidents. Any person working at the WVDP with a personal photo badge that allows unescorted access to the site’s
administrative areas must complete general employee training, which covers health and safety, emergency response, environ-
mental compliance, and other essential topics.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 3-5

 In any weather, MPPB deconstruction work was safely conducted

Additionally, the WVDP conducts an annual all-employee safety event. At the event, employees participate in hands-on activ-
ities and listen to safety lectures highlighting the importance of safety during complex deconstruction and removal work. The
2025 event was held again in September.

The WVDP’s record with respect to worker safety and the protection of the public and the environment demonstrates the suc-
cess of a well-implemented ISMS and EMS. WVCA and its subcontractors achieved 312,436 safe work hours as of December 31,
2025 without lost-time work because of accidents or illness. There were 0 hours of lost time attributed to injuries reported in
CY 2025. This safety performance was achieved while also protecting the public and the environment.

Self-Assessments

At the WVDP, self-assessment activities are stressed as a mechanism for evaluating, improving, and maintaining the safety and
protection of the public and the environment. These self-assessments are coordinated through the site Integrated Assessment
Program (IAP) and involve an annual review of each functional area of the WVDP.

Lessons Learned

The WVDP participates in the DOE Lessons Learned Program, providing internal training and opportunities to improve safety
and environmental stewardship.

Management Review

The routine annual internal review by the EMS Executive Safety Review Board (ESRB) was held in March 2025. The ESRB reviews
the site’s environmental performance annually to ensure the continuing suitability, adequacy, and effectiveness of the EMS. The
WVDP EMS was determined to operate effectively during this review.

Awards and Recognitions
The WVCA was awarded both the DOE Voluntary Protection Program (VPP) Star of Excellence and the National Safety Council
(NSC) Occupational Excellence Achievement Award for its safe work achievements in 2025. The DOE VPP Star of Excellence rec-
ognizes excellence in safety and health practices. It is the highest achievement level, awarded to contractors that demonstrate

3-6 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

outstanding safety and health programs, achieve injury and illness rates at least 75% below the national average, and meet
annual DOE VPP goals.

The Occupational Excellence Achievement Award is part of the NSC Occupational Awards Program. It aims to honor organiza-
tions that demonstrate a proactive approach to safety compliance and leadership, achieving injury and illness rates at least 50%
below the Bureau of Labor Statistics national average.

These awards are notable accomplishments for the WVDP workforce, considering the hazardous work the WVDP completed
while conducting the MPPB deconstruction. All WVDP workers have made significant contributions to the successes of 2025
and have earned these awards through hard work and dedication to maintaining our superior safety culture.

Community Outreach
Working With the Seneca Nation
The Seneca Nation is a federally recognized Native nation located approximately 17 miles downstream and to the west of the
WVDP. Because Cattaraugus Creek runs the extent of the Territory before emptying into Lake Erie, the Cattaraugus Territory
community could be potentially impacted by activities at the WVDP. This territory of the Seneca Nation is about 22,000 acres
(89 km²).

The DOE and the Seneca Nation signed a Cooperative Agreement in 1996 on the on-site low-level waste disposal as part of
the 1996 initial Environmental Impact Statement (EIS, DOE/EIS-0200-F). The Cooperative Agreement has been instrumental in
fostering the government-to-government relationship between the Seneca Nation and the DOE. The Seneca Nation’s Environ-
mental Protection Department (EPD) manages the DOE Cooperative Agreement on behalf of the Seneca Nation. The Coopera-
tive Agreement has enabled the Seneca Nation to participate in the West Valley Citizen Task Force (CTF) and the DOE State and
Tribal Government Working Group.

The proposed tasks (provided every five years in the form of a work plan and budget request) for the Cooperative Agreement
activities continue to promote communication between the Seneca Nation and the DOE regarding the Seneca Nation’s environ-
mental, human health, and cultural resources. The DOE at the WVDP also entered into a Memorandum of Agreement (MOA)
between the Seneca Nation and the DOE. The purpose of the MOA is to provide for notification to the Seneca Nation in advance
of any shipments of the WVDP spent nuclear fuel and high-level waste across the Seneca Nation lands and to provide for the
safe and secure transportation of such material, including the development of culturally sensitive risk management strategies
and emergency planning and response, in coordination with the Seneca Nation.

Community Engagement
The WVDP has worked to establish long-term relationships with local communities and strives to communicate and engage
community stakeholders regularly. The communication plan focuses on keeping internal and external stakeholders informed
about environmental work and cleanup progress. Some of these communication methods include community outreach/in-
volvement, as well as project presentations at Quarterly Public Meetings (QPM) and CTF meetings. Residents from surrounding
communities, across all segments of society, can become members of the CTF.

In 2025, WVDP participated in several annual events, including the Toys for Tots Program and a food drive for eight local food
pantries (two additional pantries received monetary donations), during the holiday season. In total, the WVDP Food Drive
has collected more than 2.5 million pounds of food since its inception in 1989. In April, the CHBWV president and the general
manager and communications director served as two of the judges at the Cattaraugus County NVIROTHON, an environmental
competition for high school students. At the ENVIROTHON, students tested their knowledge of soils, aquatics, forestry, wildlife,
and current environmental issues in NYS. In May, representatives from DOE, NYSERDA, and CHBWV presented community sup-
port checks to Mercy Flight, West Valley Fire District #1, and Bertrand Chaffee Hospital.

3-8 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

 CHAPTER 4
 ENVIRONMENTAL MONITORING
WVDP Environmental Monitoring Program (EMP) Overview
At the WVDP, on-site and off-site air and surface water, drinking water, sediment, soil, venison (white-tailed deer), fish, milk,
and food crop samples are collected as specified in the WVDP EMP. The overview of the program is summarized in Figure 4-1
on the next page. The sampling locations for each medium were carefully selected based on historical data reviews and con-
sultations with regulatory agencies. The samples are analyzed for radiological and non-radiological (chemical) constituents and
water quality parameters. Selected off-site locations (called ‘background’ or ‘control’ locations) are distant enough not to be
affected by the WVDP activities. They are typically located in the predominantly upwind direction of the site or upstream of the
site. (See Chapter 6 for the groundwater monitoring program).

A description of the sampling schedule at each location, monitoring program drivers and rationale, as well as maps showing the
sampling locations, are presented in Appendix A.

On-Site Airborne Emissions Monitoring Program
Point source emissions
The WVDP currently maintains required EPA approvals for radiological releases from three active air emission points (otherwise
referred to as “stacks”). This includes the Supernatant Treatment System (STS) stack (ANSTSTK), the Remote Handled Waste Fa-
cility (RHWF) stack (ANRHWFK), and the Replacement Ventilation System (RVS) stack (ANRVEU1). The ANRVEU1 stack was shut
down in 2022. The Container Sorting and Packaging Facility (CSPF) stack (ANCSPFK), which operated in 2025, does not require
EPA approval and operates only when packaging activities are conducted at the facility.

The WVDP also has EPA approval for radiological releases from up to 15 portable ventilation units (PVUs) at one time. Locations
of PVUs may change throughout the year depending on operational needs. Seven PVUs were active during 2025. The inset,
“On-Site Active Ventilation and Emissions Systems” on page 4-3 shows the photos of the on-site point sources at the WVDP and
describes their purposes.

Radiological air emissions are regulated by the EPA NESHAP, which specifies that total annual airborne emissions from WVDP
sources shall not exceed a dose of 10 mrem/year to any member of the public. Exhaust from each EPA-approved ventilation
system at the WVDP is continuously filtered before being released to the atmosphere. Emissions are sampled for radioactivity
in both particulate (e.g., cesium-137, strontium-90, and plutonium-239/240) and gaseous forms (e.g., iodine-129) at the build-
ing stacks, PVUs, and off-site ambient air samplers. (Iodine-129 is not sampled and analyzed for the PVUs.) The doses from
radiological air releases are evaluated and reported to the EPA in the annual NESHAP report and are discussed in Chapter 5,
“Dose Assessment“ in this report.

Possible diffuse emissions from deconstruction activities are evaluated before the actual work is performed based on the mea-
sured source terms (radioisotope inventory) within the structure using calculation methods dictated in the NESHAP regulations.
Emissions during deconstruction activities on site are monitored by the off-site ambient air monitoring network. (See Figure
A-7 in Appendix A.)

Diffuse source emissions
Diffuse emissions to air from surface water evaporation off the on-site lagoons are estimated each year based on the annual
average concentrations of radioisotopes in the lagoons.

Nonradiological Air Emissions
The WVDP maintains an Air Facility Registration Certificate and routinely evaluates new potential sources of nonradiological
emissions to determine if any new sources need to be added to the registration or if any new monitoring or permitting is re-
quired. The only emissions currently covered under the Air Facility Registration Certificate are fugitive emissions from the use of
spray foam sealants, which are far below the permitting threshold. The site’s diesel and natural gas emergency generators and
fire pumps are operated for fewer than 500 hours per year and thus are exempt from permitting. Only low-sulfur diesel fuel is
used on site. All asbestos removal activities are routinely monitored for asbestos emissions. No other nonradiological monitor-
ing or nonradiological air emissions permits are required at the WVDP.

Meteorological Monitoring
The on-site meteorological tower continuously monitors wind speed, wind direction, and temperature at the 33-ft (10-m)
and 197-ft (60-m) elevations. (See Figure A-1 for the location.) The systems are provided with backup power in the event of
site power failures. All site logs have been converted to electronic records. These data are used to assess the potential effects
of routine and nonroutine releases of airborne radioactivity and to provide input data to airborne dispersion models (e.g.,
CAP88-PC), which can be used to calculate doses to off-site residents. These data can also be used by the Emergency Response
Organization (ERO) at the WVDP to predict the direction of plume migration if an unplanned air release occurs.

Predominant wind directions in 2025 were similar to the directions recorded by the tower in previous years. Total precipitation
in 2025 was 37.29 inches (94.72 cm), approximately four inches less than the annual precipitation in 2024. (See Table 4-1.)
Comparing the 2025 monthly averages to the 10-year averages, it is apparent that the precipitation was lower than the average
in most months in 2025.

Understanding Environmental Monitoring Results
The DOE Derived Concentration Standards (DCSs), for which monitoring results are compared, are established for each radio-
isotope, and they do not exist for gross alpha and gross beta because gross results are composite values. In the data tables
in Appendices, in some cases, the DCS cells are left blank as ‘NA’, and in some cases, the DCSs for gross alpha and gross beta
are substituted with a DCS of alpha-radiation-emitting radioisotope or a beta-radiation-emitting radioisotope. (See Table 8-4.)
Listing ‘NA’ for the DCS of gross alpha and gross beta, or substituting the DCSs with those of specific alpha/beta radioisotopes,
depends on factors such as sample media (air or water) or whether the radioisotope constituents in gross alpha/beta for the
particular pathway (inhalation or ingestion) are known. Refer to the inset, “Radiological Data Evaluation,” on page 4-11 for a
detailed discussion of the DCSs and the gross alpha/beta substitution with specific isotopes.

In the following discussions of monitoring results in the Appendices tables, for the results with ‘±’, positive detections (or ‘de-
tection’) refer to the results greater than the corresponding laboratory analytical uncertainties. If a single result is presented
without ‘< (less than uncertainty)’, it indicates the positive detection, and conversely, the result indicates non-positive detection
(‘nondetect’) if the result is presented with ‘< ’.

On-Site Point-Source Monitoring Update for 2025
Tables C-1 to C-4 in Appendix C present total radioactivity released from each of the on-site air emission point sources. The
on-site air emission concentrations from the stacks were compared to the background sampling results in Great Valley, NY
(location code AFGRVAL), approximately 18 miles (29 km) south of the site. (See Figure A-14 for the background location.) This
background location has a very low potential to be affected by radiological releases from the WVDP activities as being upwind
of the site. Therefore, ambient air sampled at this location is a good indicator of radioisotope concentrations in the regional air.
Gross alpha and gross beta results from 2025 at AFGRVAL are presented in Table C-5.

T&VDS emissions (ANSTSTK): In 2025, the annual average isotope concentrations at the ANSTSTK were within the historical
range. Gross alpha, gross beta, iodine-129, cesium-137, and uranium series were detected; however, the isotopic concentra-
tions were several orders of magnitude lower than the DCSs (Table C-1). Gross beta is usually detected in ANSTSTK emissions
because of the residual beta radiation-emitting constituents (mostly cesium-137) in the WTF that ANSTSTK ventilates.

RHWF emissions (ANRHWFK): Remote size-reduction to segregate and repackage waste continued in the RHWF throughout
2025. At this stack, gross alpha and uranium-238 were detected in 2025 and the concentration levels of uranium series were
multiple orders of magnitude lower than the DCSs (Table C-2).

 (continued on page 4-6)

4-4 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

 On-Site Air Monitoring in Deconstruction Work Areas
Radioactivity in the on-site air is monitored on cameras and digital displays in an on-site control room in real time. When an
anomaly is detected, the finding is promptly communicated to on-site operational personnel. Additionally, layers of air mon-
itoring samplers are installed close to the work area to protect the health and safety of workers. These work area samplers
provide early indication of unexpected elevated readings and allow site-operational personnel to take action promptly, such
as pausing deconstruction operations, before the site workers and the public are exposed to risk.

Air monitors and samplers like the ones shown below are used at the WVDP site. Continuous Air Monitors (CAMs) placed
near the deconstruction work area detect elevated levels of radioactivity, providing early warnings to prompt necessary op-
erational adjustments. Radiation Protection personnel have continuous, direct access to the CAM readings from the opera-
tions control room. The Fixed Air Samplers (FASs) are routinely analyzed by the on-site laboratory during all worker activities.
Radiation Protection personnel also survey designated surfaces and particle deposition plates for radioactivity many times
daily during deconstruction and waste management activities.

For on-site air monitoring, specific regulations for worker protection, not environmental regulation, are applied. On-site air
monitoring in the work area is required to demonstrate compliance according to 10 CFR 835.403(a)(1) and (2), ‘Occupational
Radiation Protection.’ Some of the conditions stated in 10 CFR 835 include documenting radiological conditions and changes
in the conditions and verifying the effectiveness of engineering and process controls in containing radioactive material on site
and reducing radiation exposure to workers. The radiological control methods are specified in DOE-STD-1098-2017, “Radio-
logical Control” (Article 555.2). Sampling results are evaluated against the control level of 2% of the Annual Limit on Intake
(ALI) (0.1 rem/year), which equates to 2% of Derived Air Concentration (DAC) (2.5 mrem/hour) for 2,000 work hours (0.05
mrem/hour × 2000 hours = 0.1 rem).

Please refer to 10 CFR 835 and DOE-STD-1098-2017 for more details of radiological protection for on-site workers.

CSPF emissions (ANCSPFK): This container sorting and packaging facility operated in the first half of 2025 for the first time since
2020. Only gross alpha was detected at this point source, and its concentration was several orders of magnitude below the DCS
standards of radioisotopes (Table C-3). When this stack was last used in 2010, iodine-129 and the uranium series were positively

PVU emissions: PVUs were used in seven locations during 2025; inside the Lag Storage Area-3 (LAS-3), the Low Level Waste
Treatment System (LLW2) packaging room, and a building housing real-time on-site radioactivity monitoring room. Gross alpha,
gross beta, and uranium series were positively detected in the PVU emissions. Still, the uranium concentration levels were a few
orders of magnitude smaller than the DCS concentrations (Table C-4).

Gross alpha activity is commonly detected on on-site air monitoring filters, reflecting both residual alpha-emitting contaminants
that remain in the facilities and naturally occurring radon decay products. However, the levels detected remain well below the
guidance levels noted above.

Off-Site Ambient Air Monitoring Program
Seventeen ambient air samplers (one sampler in each of the 16 compass directions and an additional sampler in the NNW
direction) surround the WNYNSC within approximately one to two miles of the WVDP property boundary for off-site
environmental surveillance. (See Figure 4-2 on page 4-7.)

The current Ambient Air Monitoring Network (AAMN) was installed in October 2012, and routine ambient air monitoring at
these samplers began in 2013. One of the samplers is a high-volume sampler (AF16HNNW) located downwind in the prevailing
wind direction (NNW), which is the direction of the hypothetical maximum potential exposure.

The high-volume sampler (AF16HNNW) operates at a flow rate more than five times that of the other low-volume samplers and
was installed to confirm the results of the lower-volume sampling. The high-volume sampler can detect particulate radioisotopes
down to approximately 0.1% to 0.2% of the compliance level. (Although the high-volume sampler does not include a sample for
iodine-129, the co-located low-volume sampler does measure iodine-129.)

Off-site filter samples are collected biweekly for gross alpha and gross beta screening, and charcoal cartridges are collected
monthly for iodine-129 screening analysis. These samples, collected on a biweekly or monthly basis, go through an on-site
screening to promptly detect any anomaly. Then, the biweekly filters are composited quarterly and sent to an analytical laboratory
for detailed isotopic analysis. Strontium-90, iodine-129, cesium-137, uranium-232, plutonium-238, plutonium-239/240, and
americium-241 are analyzed for off-site air filters. These isotopes were known to have existed on the site when this monitoring
program was designed. The same tests are performed on samples collected at a background ambient air sampler located in
Great Valley. Both off-site and background samples include naturally occurring radioisotopes such as radon decay products that
are detected in gross alpha and beta measurements.

The off-site ambient air samplers are visited once a week for inspection and maintenance. A remote power surveillance system
monitors the power at the ambient air sampling stations. When power is lost, such as during a storm or when components
malfunction, the system automatically sends a text message to the Environmental Services (ES) air sampling technician, who
will quickly check and repair the sampling system.

The ability to respond immediately to power outages minimizes downtime, helping ensure that ambient air surrounding the
site is sampled continuously in all sectors. However, power outages could result in the loss of up to a week of air monitoring
data, particularly after a severe storm. The ambient air samplers ran 99.08 % of the year in 2025.

Off-Site Ambient Air Monitoring Update for 2025
The radioisotopic data from the ambient air samplers are used to demonstrate compliance with EPA air emissions standards for
public exposure. They are compared with the NESHAP concentration levels for environmental compliance. (For details, refer to
the “Air Emissions Compliance Obligation” section in Chapter 2.)			 (continued on page 4-8)

Surface Water Monitoring Program
The WVDP site is drained by several small streams flowing off
site. Franks Creek enters from the south and receives drain- Ambient air sampler in the north-northwest direction
age from the south plateau. As it flows northward, Franks (photo credit: S. Wedvik)
Creek is joined by Erdman Brook, which receives effluent
from the LLW treatment building (LLW2) through the lagoon system. The lagoon system receives augmentation water from
the lakes located south of the site during discharge as well. After leaving the site at the security fence, Franks Creek receives
drainage from the northeast swamp areas on the north plateau (WNSWAMP) and from Quarry Creek, which receives drainage
from the north swamp location, WNSW74A. (See Figure 4-3 for the locations.) Franks Creek then flows into Buttermilk Creek,
which, after flowing northward through the WNYNSC, enters Cattaraugus Creek. Cattaraugus Creek flows westward away from
the WNYNSC and ultimately drains into Lake Erie to the northwest. (See also Figures 1-1, 1-2, and A-14.)

The primary releases from the site to surface waters occur at three locations, the lagoon 3 weir which discharges at outfall 001
([sampling location code WNSP001] on Figure 4-3), the northeast swamp drainage ditch (WNSWAMP) by natural drainage, and
the north swamp drainage ditch (WNSW74A), also by natural drainage.

Members of the public do not have access to the WVDP premises and therefore have no potential for direct exposure to surface
water on site. The first point of public access to surface water potentially impacted by the site is on Cattaraugus Creek, down-
stream of the WVDP at Felton Bridge (location code WFFELBR), as shown in Figure 4-8 on page 4-16.

State Pollutant Discharge Elimination System (SPDES) Permit Required Monitoring
Waste Water Discharge Monitoring (Outfall 001)
Liquid discharges from the WVDP site are regulated under a NYS SPDES permit (SPDES Number: NY0000973, 2015). The permit
lists compliance points from which liquid effluents are released to Erdman Brook and specifies the sampling and analytical re-
quirements for each discharge point. The SPDES permit originally included monitoring of four wastewater discharge outfalls on
the WVDP premises; however, only one of them, outfall 001, was an active discharge point in 2025. Outfall 001 is a discharge
point of lagoon 3 water to Franks Creek.

Water used for dust suppression during the MPPB deconstruction and surface flow in the MPPB footprint area is captured by
the site water management system. Captured water is evaluated to determine whether it can be treated in the LLW treatment
system and discharged through outfall 001, or requires off-site disposal. Radioactivity in the pre-discharge from outfall 001 is
compared with DOE DCSs and submitted to NYSDEC to facilitate discharge approval, as required by the SPDES permit. Three
discharges from outfall 001 occurred in 2025, and the total annual discharge volume was 5.2 million gallons (gal) (1.98 million
L), about a 10% decrease from the 2024 discharge volume. Usually, three or four discharges from outfall 001 are conducted in
a year.

4-8 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

Stormwater Discharge Monitoring
Rain and snowmelt events flowing over land or impervious surfaces (e.g., paved streets, parking lots, and building rooftops)
generate stormwater runoff. The runoff flows on the surface, potentially transferring pollutants to surface water, groundwater,
and soil. Runoff can also move surface soils and sediment downgradient, which can lead to increased surface soil erosion and
sedimentation in creeks.

The WVDP SPDES permit lists 19 stormwater discharge outfalls as required monitoring points, in addition to the outfall 001
wastewater discharge point. (See Figures A-3 and A-4 for the outfall locations.) Appendix B-1 lists regulatory limits for chemical
constituents in discharges to surface water under the SPDES program and additional water quality and potable water stan-
dards. The amount of rainfall, the storm event duration, volume discharged, and the maximum flow rate must also be reported
at each stormwater outfall. The WVDP stormwater outfalls are grouped into eight representative drainage basins that could
potentially be influenced by deconstruction or site maintenance activity runoff. One representative outfall from each group
must be sampled on a semiannual basis. Stormwater samples are not required to be analyzed for radiological parameters under
the SPDES permit. Still, they are screened for gross alpha and gross beta radioactivity at the WVDP on-site laboratory.

Whole Effluent Toxicity (WET) Testing
The SPDES permit also includes periodic special studies, such as discharge effluent toxicity testing every five years. This test
involves sending samples of the SPDES predischarge and composite of discharged waters from the site to a bioassay laboratory
where vertebrate (fathead minnow) and invertebrate (water flea) freshwater species are tested and evaluated for survival rate,
growth rates, and rates of reproduction. In January 2024, NYSDEC agreed that WET testing at the WVDP could include only
invertebrate water flea test and testing for the fathead minnow would no longer be required, based on the test results in 2022
and 2023. 2025 WET testing results are listed in Table B-2G.

There were no SPDES effluent limit exceedances and no SPDES noncompliance events during 2025. The SPDES Discharge Moni-
toring Reports (DMRs) are posted on the WVDP-DOE website in entirety (https://www.energy.gov/wvdp/public-reading-room).

 Calculating Flow-Weighted Mean Concentrations
 Flow-weighted mean concentrations (FWMC) are concentrations that are adjusted for the variability in stream flow over a
 given period of time (e.g., monthly or annually). FWMC is useful for estimating the typical concentration of a contaminant
 adjusting for stream flow. This allows for comparisons between streams with different flow volumes between years.

 Flow-weighted mean concentration is defined as = Total Load (kg or Ci)
 Total Stream Flow Volume (m3)

 where the total load (kg or Ci) is divided by the total stream volume (m3) for a given time period (e.g., year or month). By
 calculating FWMC on a monthly or annual basis, variability due to seasonal and historical sampling frequency fluctuations
 and missing data can be reduced.

 Waterborne dose calculations use radioisotope input values in Ci, the same units used for airborne dose calculations. Ci
 values calculated using FWMC lead to an accurate waterborne dose estimate from samples collected from surface water
 bodies with variable flow volumes.

4-10 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

 Radiological Data Evaluation
  Derived Concentration Standards (DCSs). “DCSs are radiological quantities, used in the development and conduct of
  environmental and public radiation protection programs implemented at DOE sites and for DOE activities. A DCS for
  exposure to a radionuclide in a specified environmental medium (e.g., air or water) represents the concentration of
  the radionuclide in that medium that would result in an effective dose of 1 millisievert (mSv) [100 millirem (mrem)]
  to a reference member of the population based on continuous exposure for one year (e.g., the concentration in water
  consumed over a year that would cause 1 mSv),” as defined in DOE-STD-1196-2022. The intended applications of DCSs
  are as follows:

  • Defining criteria for applying Best Available Technology (BAT) at the point of discharge for liquid effluent streams (in
 accordance with DOE Order 458.1, “Radiation Protection of the Public and the Environment.”)

  • Relative ranking of the importance of radionuclides within a waste stream.

  • Relative ranking of multiple effluent streams to air or water.

  The DCSs were developed with consideration of only three exposure modes (ingestion of water, inhalation of air, and air
  submersion). In 2021, ingestion of milk was added to these exposure modes. While they provide relative guidance for the
  ranking of potential radionuclides in effluent streams released from facilities, they are not intended to be used to infer the
  dose to members of the public nor to demonstrate compliance with DOE radiation protection dose limits. “The DCS values
  represent concentrations at the point of discharge and do not account for attenuation along the pathway before reaching
  the receptor. Typically, more complex environmental pathways are involved. A detailed pathway analysis is required for
  calculating public radiation doses resulting from DOE activities.“ (DOE-STD-1196-2022).

  DCSs applicable to the radionuclides present at the WVDP are presented in Table 8-4 in Chapter 8, “Radiation and
  Environmental Standards” of this report. When only gross alpha and beta measurements are available in WVDP air sample
  results, activity is assumed to come from plutonium-239/240 (alpha) and strontium-90 (beta), respectively, because the
  DCSs for these radionuclides are the most limiting for major WVDP particulate emissions. For water effluents, when only
  gross alpha and beta measurements are available, activity is assumed to come from uranium-232 for gross alpha, and
  strontium-90 for gross beta, also because their DCSs are the most limiting for major WVDP waterborne exposures.

  Sum of Fractions. Environmental sampling results at the WVDP are assessed to determine whether the constituents
  of interest are present and, if so, their concentrations are compared with DCSs as guidelines for controlling potential
  exposure to the public. To evaluate the radioactivity released from each location with respect to the DCSs, the annual
  average radionuclide concentration measured for each nuclide was divided by its respective DCS and the fractions from
  all nuclides are summed. If the sum of the fractions (also called the “sum of ratios”) exceeds 1.0, or if expressed as the
  sum of percentages exceeds 100%, then the total radioactivity released from that location during the current year exceeds
  DCSs and further evaluation is required.

  This comparison to DCSs is a very conservative method of evaluating the concentration data because it assumes continuous
  exposure by drinking the water or inhaling the air at the point of measurement which may be at an on-site location where
  no public exposure is possible and where no water is ingested. DCSs are therefore used as a precaution to ensure releases/
  emissions are not approaching levels that could cause dose limits to be exceeded off site. The regulatory limit is based on
  the modeled/measured total annual off-site dose, not this comparison to DCSs.

  Statistical Comparison to Background. Data from near-site locations are compared with background concentrations using
  standard statistical methods to assess possible site impacts on the environment. Refer to Chapter 7, “Quality Assurance”
  and Chapter 8, “Radiation and Environmental Standards” for more discussions on measurement result analysis. Results
  from each location are also compared to the current year and/or historical data from the background locations to
  determine if any trends (e.g., increasing constituent concentrations) are occurring. (See Table 4-2.)

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 4-11

Outfall 001 Radiological Monitoring
The annual average concentrations in the outfall 001 discharge for each radioisotope are compared with the corresponding
DOE DCS concentrations, which correspond to the DOE standard of 100 mrem/year from waterborne exposure. (See the inset,
“Radiological Data Evaluation,” on page 4-11.) The concentration of each radioisotope in the sample is divided by the DCS to
obtain a fraction. Then, fractions of all analyzed radioisotopes are summed. The sum of fractions less than 1 indicates that ra-
dioisotope concentrations in outfall 001 discharge met the DOE guideline. Chapter 5, “Dose Assessment” explains applicable
regulatory compliance at the WVDP and dose calculation methods. Table B-2A shows the 2025 monitoring results at outfall 001.

Annual isotopic concentrations at outfall 001 (WNSP001) are summarized using flow-weighted mean concentrations because
of the batch nature of discharges from outfall 001. (See the inset box, “Calculating Flow-Weighted Mean Concentrations,” on
page 4-10.)

In 2025, discharge concentrations from outfall 001 were similar to those in previous years, except that the flow-weighted gross
beta concentration increased by about 32% compared with 2024 and was close to the strontium-90 DCS. The increase might
have been the result of the MPPB deconstruction work, which concluded in June 2025. The WVDP will continue to monitor the
concentration trend in 2026. The sum of fractions in 2025 was 0.29 (or 29% of the DCSs as shown in Table B-2A.) The sum of
fractions has remained at about the same level at outfall 001, around 0.25, except for increases during the COVID-19 pandemic
years due to reduced discharge activities. In 2025, gross alpha, gross beta, tritium, strontium-90, cesium-137, uranium-238,
and americium-241 measurements at outfall 001 statistically exceeded background measurements at the upstream sampling
location, WFBCBKG, on Buttermilk Creek. However, those results were orders of magnitude lower than the corresponding
DCSs, except strontium-90. (See Table 4-2 on page 4-22 for 2025 background exceedance results.)

Surface Water Discharge Monitoring
Potential Surface Water Contamination Sources
On the north plateau, possible contaminant sources, other than the planned discharges at outfall 001 and the groundwater
plume, to surface water include the Waste Tank Farm (WTF), facility deconstruction activities, the lagoon system associated
with the LLW2, and waste handling and storage facilities. North plateau surface water sampling locations that monitor these
potential sources include sampling locations WNSP005 and WNSP006, in addition to WNSWAMP and WNSW74A.

On the south plateau, the two inactive underground radioactive waste disposal areas (the NRC-licensed disposal area [NDA]
and NYS-licensed disposal area [SDA]), the 56 Vertical Storage Casks (VSCs) (stored on the interim HLW Cask Storage Pad),
the drum cell (a building formerly used to store drums of processed LLW), and waste management activities are all potential
contamination sources. Surface water drainage across the south plateau is monitored at locations WNNDADR, WNERB53, and
WNFRC67 (Figure 4-3). Surface water flow is directed around the NDA and SDA by stormwater drainage pipes, culverts, and
drop inlets.

These surface water monitoring points are sited where radiological releases from potential source areas could be detected and
are routinely sampled for pH and radiological parameters in accordance with the WVDP environmental monitoring program.

Discharge To Off-Site Creek Points
Natural surface water drainage to Franks Creek from WNSWAMP largely consists of emergent groundwater supplemented by
surface runoff. Elevated gross beta concentrations in surface water were first identified at WNSWAMP in 1993. Subsequent
investigations delineated a plume of strontium-90-contaminated groundwater on the north plateau seeping into surface water
flowing through the WNSWAMP (See Chapter 6, “Groundwater Protection Program” for details about the groundwater gross
beta/strontium-90 plume.) Surface water contaminated with strontium-90 flows from the WNSWAMP drainage ditch through
Franks Creek and Buttermilk Creek into Cattaraugus Creek, where the water is sampled at Felton Bridge (WFFELBR), the first
point of public access. Annual radiological releases at WNSWAMP are summarized using flow-weighted mean concentrations
(FWMCs) to monitor this discharge point, toward which the groundwater strontium-90 plume flows, and to account for flow
volume fluctuations.

Surface Water Discharge Update for 2025 (Other Than Outfall 001)
WNSWAMP

Total flow volume through the WNSWAMP drainage in 2025 was approximately 11.0 million gal (41.8 million
liters [L]). Gross alpha, gross beta, strontium-90, iodine-129, uranium series, and plutonium-239/240 were detect-
ed in the discharge water in 2025. However, the annual average concentrations were several orders of magnitude low-
er than the corresponding DCSs. The sum of fractions of all radioisotopes was 0.25 (or 25% of the DCSs) in 2025, the
same fraction as that in 2024. (See Table B-3A.) In 2025, gross alpha, gross beta, and strontium-90 measurements at
WNSWAMP statistically exceeded background measurements at the upstream sampling location, WFBCBKG, on Buttermilk
Creek. (See Figure A-5 for WFBCBKG location and Table 4-2 for background exceedance.) As in past years, the major contributor
to the sum of fractions at WNSWAMP was strontium-90. However, the strontium-90 concentration remained below the DCS.
The fraction of strontium-90 to the DCS was 0.2459, very close to 0.2460 in 2024.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 4-13

WNSW74A

Total flow volume through the WNSW74A drainage in 2025 was estimated to be 12.3 million gal (46.7 million L). The sum of
fractions of radioisotopes discharged from WNSW74A was 0.019 (or 1.9% of DCSs), lower than 0.023 in 2024, and was less than
10% of that observed at WNSWAMP. (See Table B-3B.)

The majority of the radioactivity at WNSW74A in 2025 was attributable to strontium-90 (0.0180 in the ratio), as it has been
historically. Besides strontium-90, gross beta, uranium-233/234, uranium-238, and americium-241 were detected at very low
levels. In 2025, gross alpha, gross beta, and strontium-90 results at WNSW74A statistically exceeded the background measure-
ments; however, the results were more than an order of magnitude below the DCS values. (See Table 4-2.)

Figure 4-4 shows a ten-year comparison of the annual average strontium-90 concentrations from WNSWAMP and WNSW74A.
As seen in this figure, the strontium-90 concentrations, mostly from the strontium-90 plume seepage, observed at WNSW74A
are much lower than the concentrations at WNSWAMP. Strontium-90 concentrations increased during the COVID-19 pandemic
years at both WNSWAMP and WNSW74A, similar to the trend at outfall 001, possibly due to reduced site activities (reduced
water usage) during that period.

Other North Plateau Surface Water Sampling Locations (WNSP005 and WNSP006)

WNSP005 is located east of the former MPPB footprint, downgradient of former lagoon 1 and other localized contamination
areas (e.g., Solvent Dike). Gross alpha, gross beta, three radioisotopes, and pH are measured at WNSP005. Lagoon 1 was one
of the five open lagoons in the LLW2 area and was contaminated with tritium. Lagoon 1 was taken out of service, backfilled,
and capped in the 1980s. In the last couple of years, gross beta concentrations at the WNSP005 drainage ditch have slightly
increased from 2.01±0.02E-06 µCi/mL in 2023 to 3.29±0.03E-06 µCi/mL in 2025. It is suspected that gross beta in lagoon 1 has
impacted the gross beta concentration in WNSP005 via groundwater migration.

In 2025, gross alpha, gross beta, and strontium-90 were detected at WNSP005 based on annual averages, consistent with pre-
vious years. The annual average concentrations of these isotopes increased compared with 2024 and exceeded the background
measurements. However, the results were within the same order of magnitude as 2024, and the average strontium-90 concen-
tration remained below the DCS. (See Table B-3C.)

WNSP006 discharges site surface water to Franks Creek on the eastern edge of the north plateau. Gross alpha, gross beta,
and 14 radioisotopes are measured at WNSP006. In 2025, gross beta, strontium-90, uranium-233/234, uranium-238, and total

The NDA groundwater interceptor trench was installed in 1991 on the northeast and northwest boundaries of the NDA to inter-
cept and collect potentially contaminated groundwater migrating from the NDA. (See Figure A-1 for the interceptor location.)
Tritium was later detected in the trench water, and monitoring of tritium at WNNDADR began in 1992. Figure 4-6 shows the
tritium concentrations in 1992 and the last 20 years. The concentrations are compared against those at the background location
on Buttermilk Creek (WFBCBKG).

Tritium concentrations at WNNDADR have decreased overall since 1992, with periodic fluctuations. When tritium monitoring
began at WNNDADR in 1992, the highest concentration level was 1.79E-05 µCi/mL. The annual average concentration of tritium
in 2025 was 4.39E-08 µCi/mL, which was less than the uncertainty. (See Table B-3E.) Since tritium’s half-life is 12.43 years, these
decreasing tritium concentrations in the last three decades are partly attributable to radioactive decay.

WNFRC67 discharges surface water in the proximity of SDA to Franks Creek. Gross beta was detected in 2025, at a similar con-
centration level to that in 2024. The detected gross beta level was close to the WFBCBKG level. It appears that naturally occurring
gross beta is detected at WNFRC67. No isotopes measured at WNFRC67 were statistically above background measurements.
Please refer to Table B-3E for WNFRC67 results and to Table B-3G for the entire list of background isotopic concentrations.)

Off-Site Surface Water Monitoring
The aerial photograph of the major creeks downstream of the site (Figure 4-7), shows the northern end of the WNYNSC where
Buttermilk Creek and Cattaraugus Creek meet. Surface water samples are collected at three off-site locations:

4-16 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

 • One location on Buttermilk Creek upstream of the WVDP.

 • One downstream location on Buttermilk Creek at Thomas Corners Bridge, just before Buttermilk Creek flows into Cat-
 taraugus Creek.

 • One further downstream location on Cattaraugus Creek at Felton Bridge, the first point of public access to surface
 water downstream of both the WNYNSC and the WVDP.
Creek water samples are collected biweekly with a continuous water sampling system and composited into monthly or
semiannual samples. Historical background samples were collected at Bigelow Bridge on Cattaraugus Creek located on Route
240 (location code WFBIGBR), upstream of the confluence of Buttermilk Creek and Cattaraugus Creek. (See Figure A-5.)
Historical data from WFBIGBR from 1991 through 2007 have been used to establish upstream background concentrations
for Cattaraugus Creek to compare them with the samples collected at WFFELBR. All other on-site and off-site surface water
monitoring data are compared with the current year’s monitoring results at WFBCBKG. Off-site creek water sampling is timed
and continuously collected every 20 minutes (10 ml) for two weeks, composited, and analyzed for gross alpha, gross beta,
tritium, strontium-90, and cesium-137 radioactivity.

2025 Update for Off-Site Surface Water Monitoring
Thomas Corners Bridge (WFBCTCB). Sampling point WFBCTCB on Buttermilk Creek is within the WNYNSC property and down-
stream of the WVDP. The composite samples of the three radioisotopes are collected semiannually, and gross alpha and gross
beta are analyzed monthly at this location. In 2025, gross beta collected at WFBCTCB statistically exceeded the background
concentrations as in previous years. The maximum gross beta concentration at WFBCTCB (9.45E-09 µCi/mL) was 0.56% of the
strontium-90 DCS (Table B-3F). Gross beta is largely naturally occurring, mostly from geological sources and upland soil erosion
or mining, etc., and is typically detected in samples both upstream and downstream of the WVDP.

Felton Bridge (WFFELBR). Sampling point WFFELBR is a favorite launch point for kayakers and the first public access point
to Cattaraugus Creek downstream of the WVDP. The composite samples of the three radioisotopes are compiled monthly at
WFFELBR in 2025, and analysis results are tabulated in Table B-3F. Only gross beta was detected in 2025 and exceeded
the historical background measurements at WFBIGBR, as in previous years. The maximum beta concentration at WFFELBR
(5.23E-09 µCi/mL) was 0.31% of the Strontium-90 DCS.

Gross beta detected at these off-site surface water monitoring sites was in the same order of magnitude as the gross beta
concentrations at the background sampling location (WFBCBKG) in 2025 (1.82±0.86E-09 µCi/mL) and at the historical upstream
background location (WFBIBGR) (2.64±1.35E-09 µCi/mL).

Sediment and Soil Monitoring Program
Airborne particulates may be deposited onto soil by wind or precipitation. Particulate matter in streams can adsorb radiological
constituents in liquid effluents and settle on the stream bottom as sediment. Soil and sediment may subsequently be eroded or
resuspended, especially during periods of high winds or high stream flow. Those soil particles provide a pathway for radiological
constituents to reach humans either directly via exposure or indirectly through the food and water ingestion pathway.

Soil and sediment samples were last collected in 2022 and are scheduled for their next collection in 2027 as part of the five-
year schedule. On-site sediment/soil samples were collected at three locations on the north plateau where drainage has the
potential to be contaminated. Those three locations are SNSP006, SNSWAMP, and SNSW74A (Figure A-2). Soil samples are
also collected off-site at one background location (SFGRVAL, shown on Figure A-14) and at three off-site locations coinciding
with the off-site air sampling locations (SFRSPRD, SFFXVRD, and SFRT240, shown on Figure A-5). Additional off-site sediment
samples are collected at one background location on Buttermilk Creek (SFBCSED) and at three downstream locations: one on
Buttermilk Creek (SFTCSED) and two on Cattaraugus Creek (SFCCSED and SFSDSED) (Figure A-5).

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 4-17

 Per- and Polyfluoroalkyl Substances (PFAS) Sampling

 A group of man-made chemicals known as per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) were first manufactured in the
 1940s. PFAS include chemicals such as perfluorooctanoic acid (PFOA), which is used to make coatings for items such as
 water-repellent clothing and heat-resistant nonstick cookware. Another compound, perfluorooctane sulfonate (PFOS), was
 used in fire-fighting foam. The reason why PFAS are known as “forever chemicals” is that they do not break down easily and
 bioaccumulate over time. PFAS have been detected in surface water and groundwater across the country and are linked
 to cancer, thyroid disease, and other health ailments. The EPA designated PFAS as one of the emerging contaminants of
 concern in 2016, and the Agency for Toxic Substances and Disease Registry (ATSDR) designated PFAS as a public health
 concern.
 DOE Actions

 Following the EPA designation, the DOE, led by the Office of Environment, Health, Safety, and Security (EHSS), conducted its
 initial assessment of the PFAS presence at the DOE sites, including nuclear sites. An initial historical site information survey
 at the WVDP suggests that PFOA, PFOS, or other PFAS substances were not used or produced at the WVDP. In December
 2024, a DOE memo, “Managing Emerging Environmental Issues at the Department of Energy,” was signed and relieved the
 DOE of its responsibility to define PFAS-bearing wastes. However, DOE sites continue to follow applicable state and federal
 regulations pertaining to PFAS storage and disposal.

 EPA Final Ruling
 The WVDP began sampling and analysis for PFOA, PFOS, and 1,4-dioxane in January 2021 under the direction of the
 Cattaraugus County Health Department (CCHD), New York. In April 2024, the EPA established legally enforceable levels,
 called Maximum Contaminant Levels (MCLs), for six categories of PFAS in drinking water: PFOA, PFOS, PFHxS, PFNA, and
 HFPO-DA as contaminants with individual MCLs, and PFAS mixtures containing at least two or more of PFHxS, PFNA, HFPO-
 DA, and PFBS using a Hazard Index MCL to account for the combined and co-occurring levels of these PFAS in drinking water.

 New York State Actions

 In August 2020, NYSDOH adopted a drinking water MCL of 10 parts per trillion (ppt) for PFOA and PFOS and 1.0 parts per
 billion (ppb) for 1,4-dioxane and issued requirements that water supply systems begin sampling for these constituents.
 To ensure consistency in sampling, analysis, reporting, and assessment of PFAS, the NYSDEC’s Division of Environmental
 Remediation (DER) has developed a sampling and analysis guidance document that summarizes currently accepted
 procedures to be used for all PFAS sampling plan. The guidance was submitted to NYSDEC under 6 NYCRR Part 375 remedial
 programs. (https://www.dec.ny.gov/docs/remediation_hudson_pdf/pfassampanaly.pdf).

 PFAS Sample Analysis
 Currently, more than a few PFAS analytical methods exist, and each method is tailored for a particular matrix (media),
 utilizing a specific analytical technology. The PFAS samples collected at the WVDP are analyzed using the method, EPA 533,
 for drinking water at an EPA 533-certified analytical laboratory. The WVDP’s contracted laboratory analyzed drinking water
 for 25 PFAS substances and PFAS mixtures in 2025. For more information, please refer to the websites below.

 EPA
 https://www.epa.gov/pfas
 https://www.epa.gov/sdwa/and-polyfluoroalkyl-substances-pfas
 DOE
 https://www.energy.gov/pfas/pfas-and-polyfluoroalkyl-substances
 https://extapps.dec.ny.gov/docs/remediation_hudson_pdf/pfassampanaly.pdf
 New York State
 https://dec.ny.gov/environmental-protection/site-cleanup/pfas

4-18 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

Drinking Water Monitoring Program
The WVDP maintains a nontransient noncommunity drinking water supply that is used only by site employees, primarily for
showering. Project drinking water (potable water) and industrial water have been supplied by two bedrock wells since 2014,
and the wells are monitored as part of the source water protection and drinking water monitoring plans. Additionally, bottled
water is provided to site employees for consumption. Supplemental water needed for SPDES flow augmentation is supplied by
the lakes south of the MPPB.

Drinking water continues to be monitored for both radiological and chemical constituents. Monitoring is conducted at the
distribution entry point and at several site tap water locations to verify compliance with EPA, NYSDOH, and CCHD regulations.
In 2021, the WVDP began monitoring per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) compounds for the site’s drinking water per
CCHD requirements. Well water is also analyzed at three wells (WNCT272, WNEHMKE, and WWCOURT) located downgradient
of the drinking water wells to monitor the underground plume flow direction.

2025 Drinking Water Update
Monitoring results from 2025 indicated that the WVDP site’s drinking water continued to remain below the local, state, and
federal Maximum Contaminant Levels (MCLs) and drinking water standards for chemical contaminants. Radiological measure-
ments for the two supply wells and the nearby bedrock wells were similar to previous years, a few orders of magnitude smaller
than the DCSs. The sampling results for the potable water supply system and sentinel well water are presented in Appendix B-4.

In 2025, according to the updated EPA guidance on PFAS compounds in the drinking water supply issued in 2024 (described in
the inset on the previous page), one drinking water supply well on the WVDP premises was sampled in accordance with the
sampling schedule set by the CCHD. The results for six PFAS compounds and 1,4-dioxane, with individual MCLs, are reported in
Tables B-4I and B-4J. Additionally, a hazard index was calculated for PFAS mixtures containing at least two or more of perfluoro-
hexanesulfonic acid (PFHxS), perfluorononanoic acid (PFNA), hexafluoropropylene oxide dimer acid (HFPO-DA), and perfluoro-
butane sulfonic acid (PFBS) (Table B-4J). The sampling results were all nondetects, and the hazard index was 0.2, less than the
MCL violation threshold of 1.

Food Source Monitoring Program
Food samples are collected from locations near the site
and from remote locations (‘background’ or ‘control’
locations) for comparison purposes. The near-site sam-
ples are collected at the locations where the WVDP’s site
activities could potentially impact plants and animals
(Figure A-11). Background samples are collected far from
the site at locations where no contamination from the
site could occur (Figure A-14).

Corn, apples, and beans are collected at harvest time
 A TLD on the site fence
(>1 kg or 2.2 lb). Venison (deer meat) samples are typical-
ly collected during the fall when deer are most active, and fish may be collected at any time of the year but are not usually
collected during the winter. Only the edible portions of the deer and fish are sampled and analyzed for radionuclides. Milk
and venison samples are collected every year, and fish, apples, beans, and corn are collected every five years. They were last
sampled in 2022.

Measurement data from 2025 for milk and venison samples are provided in Appendix E. Dose assessment from food sources is
discussed in the “Dose from Food Samples” section in Chapter 5, “Dose Assessment.”

Direct Radiation Monitoring
Thermoluminescent Dosimeters (TLDs) directly measure radiation (mostly gamma and beta radiation) in the environment. On
the WVDP site, TLDs are placed at selected solid waste management units (SWMUs) and on the WVDP security fence. Off-site
TLDs are placed surrounding the WNYNSC perimeter (one in approximately each compass direction), on the access road, and
in Great Valley, the background sampling location. On-site TLD locations are shown in Figure A-12, and the locations of TLDs
near the WNYNSC perimeter are shown in Figure A-13. The location of the TLD in Great Valley is shown in Figure A-14. TLDs are
collected and measured twice a year with the unit of mR (milliroentgen per quarter). No changes were made to the locations
of TLDs this year.

Direct Radiation Update for 2025
Figure 4-8 presents the average exposure rate per hour for the year (the unit converted to microroentgen [µR] per hour) over
the last 10 years at the WNYNSC perimeter and the background location in Great Valley. Figure 4-8 shows the results in µR/hr
because of the small values when the monitoring results are converted to the hourly unit, corresponding to the on-site posting
rate of 5 mrem/hour.) An average exposure rate in 2025 in the Great Valley location (TLD #23) was about 16.1 mR/quarter, sim-
ilar to the exposure rate in 2024. (See ‘Background DFTLD23’ column in Figure F-1.) The annual average exposure rates in 2025
at the WNYNSC perimeter (TLDs #1-16 and 20) were 13 to 16 mR/quarter. (See the ‘Location Average’ column in Figure F-1.)
The average results at the WNYNSC perimeter TLDs have always been slightly lower than the background TLD measurements.
It is assumed that TLD measurements are higher at the background location due to geological characteristics of the area, such
as natural uranium, thorium, and radon. No particular temporal trends have been observed for either data group except for a
slight increase in background measurements in 2017. All 2025 TLD data are presented in Appendix F in units of milliroentgen
(mR)/quarter.

The exposure rates measured on the on-site TLDs (TLDs #24-44) are usually higher than the off-site TLD measurements due to
the site activities (Appendix F-2). In 2025, elevated exposure rates were observed on site at TLDs #24, #35, #38, and #40; similar
locations on the north plateau that have had elevated rates in previous years.

  • #24: On the northern edge of the site fence.

  • #35: Located near the Drum Cell and near the on-site rail line where the packaged wastes are staged before off-site
 shipment.

4-20 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

  • #38: Located on the west side of the MPPB, where packages with elevated radioactivity are staged before packing and
 off-site shipment.

  • #40: Located near the WTF where high-level liquid waste was stored previously, and the ventilating system is operating.

In 2025, the average exposure rates at TLDs #24 and #35 were approximately 1.8 times that of the background. The highest
on-site radiation exposure rate, 534±36 mR/quarter, measured in the first quarter of 2025, was at TLD #40 near the WTF, as in
the past few years.

Notably, at TLDs #35 (near the Drum Cell) and #38 (west side of the MPPB), the exposure rates in the second half of the year
drastically decreased compared with the first half of the year, to about half at #35 and to a quarter at #38. The exposure rate
at the TLD #40 also decreased in the second half. These reductions at the specific locations on the site reflect the decreased
radioactive emissions from facility deconstruction, waste processing, and shipping activities in 2025 after the completion of
MPPB deconstruction.

The Relative Percent Difference (RPD) was calculated to determine whether the direct exposure rates at each TLD were sta-
tistically higher than those at the background location (TLD #23). Direct exposures at TLDs #20, #24, #35, #38, and #40 were
statistically higher than the exposure rates at TLD #23 in 2025 (Table 4-2). TLD #20 is located near the WNYNSC perimeter in the
northwestern direction. Although the results at these TLD locations were higher than background, the values were well below
the 5 mrem/hour rate requiring on-site posting and within the DOE occupational dose limit (5 rem/year) for the TEDE (Note: 1
roentgen = 0.877 rem).
