WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 4-23

4-24 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

 CHAPTER 5
 DOSE ASSESSMENT
WVDP Dose Assessment Overview
The WVDP assesses doses to the public and the surrounding environment to demonstrate its compliance with DOE Order
458.1 and EPA NESHAP standards. Our dose monitoring program consists of three major components: 1) the assessment of
airborne dose to off-site residents based on the radioisotope activities captured on ambient air monitoring filters, 2) the assess-
ment of waterborne dose to off-site residents based on the radioisotope concentrations in on-site discharge points and off-site
streams and Lake Erie, and 3) the assessment of dose to biota (animals and plants) living on and near the site. The dose from
the on-site groundwater strontium-90 plume is not included in the WVDP dose assessment. Strontium-90 seepage modeling
conducted by the WVDP in the 2000s estimated that the majority of the plume drains into surface water streams (WNSWAMP
and WNSW74A). The seepage not captured in these surface discharge points is monitored at the off-site surface water sampling
points at WFBCTCB and WFFELBR on Cattaraugus Creek as part of surface water monitoring.

Dose Compliance Drivers

DOE Order 458.1, “Radiation Protection of the Public and the Environment,” establishes requirements to protect the public
and the environment against undue risk from radiation. Deliberate efforts are made at every level of the work at the WVDP
to protect on-site workers and also protect the public and the environment. DOE-HDBK-1216-2015, Chg 1, “Environmental
Radiological Effluent Monitoring and Environmental Surveillance,” provides acceptable methods for designing, implementing,
and assessing effluent (air and waterborne) monitoring and environmental surveillance. The DOE sets the annual dose limits of
100 mrem (1 mSv) for an off-site individual and 100 person-rem for the population in a 50-mile (80-km) radius, from all path-
ways, including airborne and waterborne sources and direct exposure.

Airborne Dose

The EPA regulates airborne radionuclide emissions under the Clean Air Act (CAA). The DOE facilities are subject to 40 CFR
61, Subpart H, National Emission Standards for Hazardous Air Pollutants (NESHAP), which is part of the CAA and contains the
national standards for emissions of radionuclides other than radon from DOE facilities. The applicable standard is a maximum
of 10 mrem (0.1 mSv) EDE to any member of the public in any year. At WVDP, compliance with NESHAP is determined by
comparing the annual average of each radionuclide concentration at each of the ambient monitoring network samplers to the
values in 40 CFR Part 61, Appendix C, Table C-6. The table lists a concentration limit for each radionuclide that would lead to
10 mrem/year dose via inhalation. A measured concentration value of each radioisotope is divided by the corresponding value
listed in Table C-6. If the sum of the fractions of all radioisotopes is less than 1, NESHAP compliance is demonstrated.

To demonstrate NESHAP compliance, the WVDP utilizes CAP88-PC v4.1, an EPA-approved airborne dose and risk modeling
software, to calculate the annual airborne doses to an individual (MEOSI) and population (collective) within a 50-mile (80-km)
radius.

Radioisotope concentrations from on-site point sources, such as stacks and ventilators, are evaluated against the DOE Derived
Concentration Standard (DCS) 2022 values, which would lead to the 100 mrem/year dose through a particular pathway.
Emissions from the on-site point sources to the surrounding environment are captured on the ambient air samplers as part of
the ambient air monitoring.

Waterborne Dose

For on-site surface water, an annual average concentration of a particular radioisotope is compared against the DOE DCS con-
centration. Radioisotopes in on-site surface water eventually migrate into creeks and become part of the off-site waterborne

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 5-1

dose. For the off-site waterborne dose, individual and population doses are calculated using GENII v2.10.2b, a DOE-approved
environmental dosimetry software. And the calculated annual doses are added to the annual airborne individual and popula-
tion doses and the total doses are compared against the 100 mrem/year standard.

Biota Dose

The biota dose standard is provided in DOE-STD-1153-2019, “A Graded Approach for Evaluating Doses to Aquatic and
Terrestrial Biota.” The WVDP uses RESRAD-BIOTA v1.8 software, a DOE-approved software to calculate doses from ionizing
radiation to the on-site and nearby populations of aquatic animals, riparian animals, terrestrial plants, and terrestrial animals.
The DOE sets daily exposure standards as <1.0 rad/d for aquatic animals, <0.1 rad/d for riparian animals, <1.0 rad/d for
terrestrial plants, and <0.1 rad/d for terrestrial animals. If the modeled doses using annual average radioisotope
concentrations in soil and surface water are less than the standards, compliance is demonstrated for biota dose.

Please refer to the following sections for details of dose modeling and the resulting doses in 2025 on each medium.

Sources of Radiation
Members of the public are routinely exposed to natural and artificial ionizing radiation that can be absorbed by living tissue.
Potential radiation exposure pathways include: inhalation of gases and particulates, ingestion of locally grown food products,
drinking water, and external exposure from contaminated materials, as shown in Figure 5-1. Various environmental media act
as pathways that transfer radionuclides from a source to a receptor.

In 2006, a typical individual living in the U.S. was estimated to receive an average annual effective dose equivalent (EDE) of
about 620 mrem (6.2 mSv) (National Council on Radiation Protection and Measurements [NCRP] Report 160, 2009). Members
of the public typically receive approximately 310 mrem/year of radiation from natural background sources, including cosmic
radiation (from outer space), terrestrial radiation, and radon (from the earth’s subsurface). Th e re maining 310 mrem/year
is from artificial sources, including consumer products and medical diagnostic procedures. (See Chapter 8, “Radiation and
Environmental Standards,” of this report for discussions of ionizing radiation.) An update of the medical exposure section of
NCRP Report No. 160 (NCRP Report No. 184 [2019]) indicates there has been a 15-20% reduction in the non-therapeutic medical
radiation dose to the U.S. population in the decade between 2006 and 2016. This reduction effectively reduces the NCRP Report
No. 160 estimate to about 560 to 580 mrem (5.6 to 5.8 mSv).

At the WVDP, sources of radioactivity could be the building materials of the former MPPB footprint, stacks and ventilators,
tanks and lagoons treating and storing wastewater, and contaminated soil on site; all from spent fuel reprocessing activities in
the 60s and 70s. These sources could be dispersed during deconstruction, excavation, and stream discharge activities. Table 5-1
summarizes the potential exposure pathways from the WVDP site to the local off-site population and describes the rationale
for including or excluding each pathway when calculating dose from the WVDP. (See the inset on page 5-5 for discussions of
“Radiation Dose” and “Units of Dose Measurement.”)

The estimated maximum individual dose from the WVDP activities in 2025 was 0.009 mrem (0.00009 mSv) including airborne
and waterborne doses. Figure 5-2 on page 5-4 shows the estimated dose from the WVDP in 2025 compared with the average
annual dose a U.S. resident receives from artificial and natural background sources.

Sample Analysis and Background Corrections
The WVDP uses the environmental monitoring measurement results returned from qualified analytical laboratories for statisti-
cal analysis and dose calculations. Analytical results returned from these laboratories include measured activity, 95% confidence
level (1.96 sigma) uncertainty, Minimum Detectable Concentration (MDC), data qualifier, and reporting limit. Instrumental back-
ground is subtracted from the result.

The WVDP does not correct measurement results for the natural background radioactivity. Also, isotopic results are not cor-
rected for locational background radioactivity (e.g., the WVDP does not subtract off-site airborne radioactivity from on-site air-
borne activity.) Instead of applying background corrections, the WVDP conducts statistical analysis and compares the on-site and
near-site results against those from off-site samples (‘background’ or ‘control’), which are not affected by the WVDP activities.

 Radiation Dose
The energy released from a radionuclide is eventually deposited in matter encountered along
the path of the radiation. The ionizing radiation energy absorbed by a unit mass of material is
referred to as the absorbed dose. The absorbing material can be either inanimate matter or
living tissue.

Alpha particles are unable to penetrate the human body beyond the skin layer but leave a dense
track of ionization in human cells as they travel through tissue and thus deliver the most dose, or harm, per unit path-
length. Because alpha particles are not penetrating, they must be taken into the body by inhalation or ingestion to cause
harm to internal organs. Beta and gamma radiation can penetrate the protective skin layer from the outside, resulting in
exposure of the internal organs to radiation.

Considering the different biological effects of different kinds of radiation, the absorbed dose is multiplied by a quality
factor specific to the isotope to yield a unit called the dose equivalent. A radiation dose expressed as a dose equivalent,
rather than as an absorbed dose, allows for the comparison of risks from different types of radiation exposure (e.g.,
exposure to alpha radiation versus exposure to gamma radiation). For this reason, regulatory agencies limit the dose to
individuals in terms of total dose equivalent. Refer to the Chapter 8, “Radiation and Environmental Standards,” for
further discussion of ionizing radiation.

 Units of Dose Measurement
The unit for dose equivalent commonly used in the U.S. is the rem. The international unit of dose equivalent is the sievert
(Sv), which is equal to 100 rem. The millirem (mrem) and millisievert (mSv), used more frequently to report the low dose
equivalents encountered in environmental exposures, are equal to one-thousandth of a rem or sievert, respectively.

The effective dose equivalent (EDE), also expressed in units of rem or Sv, provides a means of combining unequal organ
and tissue doses into a single “effective” whole body dose that represents a comparable risk probability. The effective
dose is a weighted sum of equivalent (organ-specific) doses to radiosensitive tissues, representing the relative contribu-
tion of the different tissues to the total risk for the idealized case of uniform irradiation of the whole body (DOE STD-1196-
2022). The probability that a given dose will result in the induction of a fatal cancer is referred to as the risk (‘cancer risk’)
associated with that dose. In this document, for waterborne releases, the EDE is calculated by dosimetry models recom-
mended by the International Commission on Radiological Protection (ICRP) in Publications 26, 30, 48, and 56 through 72
(as stated in the GENII version 2 User’s Guide) and risk factors in Federal Guidance Report (FGR) 15 (2019). For airborne
emissions, the EDE calculation is based on the ICRP Report Number 72 (1995) and risk factors in FGR 13 (2019). Nuclear
decay data are based on the ICRP Publication 107 (2008) (Nuclear Wallet Cards [October 2023]).

A collective population dose is expressed in units of person-rem or person-sievert because the individual doses are
summed over the entire potentially exposed population (persons). The 50-mile (80-km) radius collective dose is the sum
of all doses to all individual members of the public within 50 miles of the WVDP. Dividing the person-rem by the popula-
tion gives an average person-rem per person.

 DOE Ionizing Dose Ranges Chart
The DOE Office of Public Radiation Protection issued a chart that provides an “order of magnitude” reference for various
levels of radiation exposure (December 2017). This comprehensive chart provides perspective on the estimated dose
from the WVDP compared to other types of radiation dose familiar to the public, such as the dose used for cancer
treat-ments. (See Figure 8-1 in the Chapter 8, “The DOE Ionizing Dose Ranges Chart” of this report to view the chart.)

hypothetical radionuclide concentrations that would result in a
10 mrem/year dose if a person were exposed to that concentra-

tration of each isotope against the EPA standard for the isotope
is calculated, and the fractions of all isotopes are summed by
compass sector. Compliance is demonstrated if the sum of these
fractions for all the isotopes measured at each sampling location
is less than 1 (the annual 10 mrem dose standard.)

2025 Maximum Airborne Dose to an Off-Site Individual

During the first quarter of 2025, a very low level of positive detection was observed at one of the AAMN samplers in the north-
eastern direction. The detected iodine-129 concentration was less than the minimum detectable activity (MDA). An annual
average concentration at this sampler was nondetect, therefore, the off-site annual airborne dose in 2025 was zero (Table 5-2).
The largest fraction of isotope concentration against the NESHAP standard in 2025 was 0.046, 4.6% of the NESHAP 10 mrem
limit, in the northern direction (Table C-6). This fraction was based on the analytical uncertainty value since the annual results at
any of the sampler locations did not have positive results (in other words, for all annual average results, uncertainty values were
larger than measured results.) These results demonstrate that the WVDP was in compliance with EPA regulations for airborne
radionuclide emissions in 2025.

5-6 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

 Airborne Dose Estimates Using CAP88-PC at the WVDP

  History. The CAP88-PC model is widely used for evaluating doses and radiological risks when planning work activities
  that have the potential to release airborne radioactivity. This model estimates the human absorbed dose via ingestion,
  inhalation, air immersion, and ground surface pathways. The resulting dose is then used to demonstrate compliance with
  40 CFR 61, Subpart H (the NESHAP regulations). Version 4.1.1 of CAP88-PC (Trinity Engineering Associates, Inc., June 2021)
  is the most recent version approved by the EPA for demonstrating NESHAP compliance. However, any previous versions
  approved by the EPA can also be used for compliance.

  Previously, doses from airborne radioactive materials released from stacks and diffuse sources on the WVDP property
  were modeled using CAP88-PC to demonstrate compliance with the NESHAP. The off-site ambient air monitoring network
  (AAMN) was installed in 2012 in anticipation of future structure deconstruction work. In the following year, the dose
  estimate based on measurements at the AAMN samplers was compared against the dose estimate based on on-site sources
  to test the validity of the AAMN measurement method. The EPA reviewed both methods and granted the WVDP final
  approval to use the AAMN measurements to evaluate compliance with the 10 mrem NESHAP standard, as outlined in 40
  CFR 61, subpart H, Appendix E concentration values. A sum of fractions is calculated by dividing annual concentrations for
  each measured isotope by the Appendix E concentration value and summing these fractions for all the isotopes measured.
  The sum of fractions of <1 demonstrate compliance with the NESHAP standard. The EPA’s final approval for using the AAMN
  measurement to demonstrate the NESHAP compliance was issued in 2014.

  Until September 2022, when the MPPB deconstruction began, most of the concentrations of radioisotopes measured at
  the AAMN samplers were nondetects (below the detection limits, or the analytical uncertainty was larger than the result).
  To make conservative dose estimates from mostly nondetect results, the WVDP used the larger value of the result and
  its uncertainty from the prevalent wind direction (NNW [north-northwest]) and applied CAP88 dose factors to them. For
  uncertainty associated with radiological analysis, refer to Chapter 8, “Radiation and Environmental Standards.”

  During the MPPB deconstruction work, very low positive detections were observed on the AAMN samplers, and the dose
  modeling method was updated to estimate annual doses more realistically. The WVDP will continue to use this actual
  positive detection method for ambient air dose modeling. An overview of this updated process is described below.

  Individual dose calculation method. CAP88-PC was used to calculate a unit dose conversion factor (UDCF) at each
  AAMN location (compass direction and distance), using site-specific five-year average meteorological data. (See
  Chapter 4, “Meteorological Monitoring” section.) With site-specific meteorological data, CAP88-PC calculates radiological
  concentration, dose, and dispersion (CHI/Q) factors given unit (1) input for each isotope at all AAMN locations. The
  calculated dose conversion factor is then used to compute the dose at each AAMN location based on the annual average
  concentration of each isotope at all locations. If positive detections are observed for at least one isotope at a given AAMN
  location, the resulting doses are summed to achieve the annual total dose at that AAMN location.

  Population dose calculation method. Because positive detections may be recorded at AAMN samplers in specific compass
  directions, the WVDP population dose calculation adopted direction-based methodology. Using the CAP88-PC dispersion
  factors, a site source-term in curies (Ci) can be determined for locations where positive detections were observed. With the
  source term established for a given isotope at the AAMN location, the population dose can be calculated for the directional
  sector through a 50-mile (80-km) radius. Population dose values are then summed for all sectors, where positive results
  were observed, and for all isotopes to produce the annual collective population dose out to a radius of 50 miles from the
  site.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 5-7

Collective Population Dose. The annual collective population dose is the sum of the dose to each individual living within
50-mile (80-km) radius. This population receives approximately 511,907 person-rem/year from natural sources, calculated
by multiplying 310 mrem/year (the individual annual dose from natural sources) by 1,651,312 people living within a 50-mile
radius. The DOE-HDBK-1216-2015, “Environmental Radiological Effluent Monitoring and Environmental Surveillance,” sets a
100 person-rem collective effective dose to the affected population as a guideline for the need to perform routine environmen-
tal pathway surveillance. Because the off-site ambient airborne dose was zero in 2025, the collective dose to the population
was also zero in 2025.

Dose from Waterborne Releases
Surface Water Standards

Currently, there are no waterborne dose standards set by the EPA or DOE for an individual (MEOSI). DOE Order
458.1, “Radiation Protection of the Public and the Environment,” requires DOE facilities to limit annual radio-
logical exposure to the public from all pathways to less than 100 mrem. For the population dose, the DOE sets
100 person-rem as a guideline, or a threshold, that a facility needs to perform routine environmental pathway surveillance.

Drinking Water Standards

EPA standards establish limits on the radiation dose to the members of the public from liquid effluents through the National
Primary Drinking Water Regulations (NPDWR) (40 CFR Part 141). NYSDOH sets the limits for community water supplies in the
New York State Sanitary Code (10 NYCRR 5-1). The EPA and NYSDOH drinking water limit is 4 mrem/year.

Waterborne Dose Assessment Methodology

Potential doses to near-site residents and the local population from the waterborne pathway are estimated using software,
GENII v2, a DOE-approved dose modeling software. The WVDP uses a chronic surface exposure model provided by GENII. The
dose from the WVDP activities through the water pathway to a hypothetical MEOSI is estimated at Felton Bridge, the first point
of public access to Cattaraugus Creek downstream of the WVDP. For the waterborne collective dose, the total dose is estimated

In waterborne dose modeling, annual averages of radioisotope concentra-

of fish from Cattaraugus Creek, followed by the consumption of vegetables and milk. The major source of waterborne exposure
for the population is fish, followed by the consumption of vegetables and fruits.

2025 Potential Maximum Waterborne Dose to an Off-Site Individual
The characteristics of isotope concentrations in discharges from SPDES outfall 001 (lagoon 3 discharges) and natural discharges
from WNSW74A and WNSWAMP remained similar to those in previous years. The radioactivity of the discharged isotopes
was multiplied by the Unit Dose Factors (UDFs) to calculate the dose to the MEOSI and the dose to the population. (See the
inset, "Using Dose Conversion Factors to Estimate Waterborne Dose," on page 5-10 for waterborne dose modeling.)
Distributions of radioisotope contributions in the waterborne dose in 2025 are summarized in Figure 5-4. As in previous years,
strontium-90 and cesium-137 were the two major sources of waterborne dose.

The fraction of strontium-90 and cesium-137 in the waterborne dose has decreased compared with those in 2022 and 2023:
approximately a 15% decrease for strontium-90 and about a 5% decrease for cesium-137. These changes were the result of the
waterborne dose model update effort to reflect the latest isotope concentrations in discharge water, Cattaraugus Creek flow
data, and regional food consumption and production data.

In the waterborne dose generated from the outfall 001, the controlled discharge point, strontium-90 contributed approximately
57% of the dose in 2025, followed by cesium-137, which contributed 30% of the dose. In the waterborne dose generated
from natural drainages, strontium-90 contributed 77% at WNSWAMP and 21% at WNSW74A. At both natural drainage points,
carbon-14 contributed more than cesium-137 to their total doses. The fractions of cesium-137 contributions to total doses at
these two locations were only 1% at WNSWAMP and 3% at WNSW74A. Carbon-14 emits low-energy beta radiation and is pro-
duced naturally in the atmosphere and during nuclear energy generation and nuclear weapons tests. The annual mean concen-
trations of carbon-14 at the three discharge points were about four orders of magnitude smaller than the DCS concentration.

The concentrations of discharged isotopes and flow volumes at the waterborne dose modeling locations are reported in
Tables B-2A, B-3A, and B-3B.

2025 Collective Waterborne Population Dose
The collective dose to the population living within a 50-mile (80-km) radius of the WVDP was 0.1 person-rem (0.001 person-Sv)
in 2025. The dose is about one order larger than those in past years. The increase stemmed from the UDF update that
included the latest Cattaraugus Creek flow data and the food consumption and production data in the region. Despite the
increase, the person-rem is still 0.1% of the annual 100 person-rem limit set by the DOE.

Radon
According to the NCRP, on average, naturally occurring radon (radon-222) and thoron (radon-220) contribute 37%
(228 mrem) of the total natural and artificial dose to a member of the public (Figure 5-2). Thoron (radon-220) has his-
torically been detected in the airborne emissions from the WVDP due to the thorium reduction extraction (THOREX)
process that was performed in the MPPB under NFS operations. Thoron levels increased during the startup of HLW

 Using Dose Conversion Factors To Estimate Waterborne Dose
  The environmental dosimetry computer code, GENII version 2.10.2b, is used to calculate site-specific Unit Dose Factors
  (UDFs) for waterborne radioactivity releases and dispersion of these effluents from the WVDP. The annual waterborne dose
  from the WVDP activities is estimated by multiplying the radioactivity in curies discharged from on-site discharge points by
  their respective isotope UDFs, and summing the dose contribution from each isotope.

  The annual individual (MEOSI) dose and collective (population) dose were calculated using the site-specific parameters as
  well as GENII recommended parameters. Those parameters include, but are not limited to: local food consumption data,
  local farming/food production data, water sports activities in the area, soil characteristics, and climate data. The MEOSI
  dose modeling point is at Felton Bridge on Cattaraugus Creek, the first public access point to the creek water downstream of
  the WVDP site. The collective dose was calculated using the estimated isotopic concentrations in Cattaraugus Creek at the
  Main Bridge in Gowanda, NY, for the population (approximately 1.6 million) within a 50-mile (80-km) radius of the WVDP.

  The annual average concentrations of three isotopes at three on-site discharge points (outfall 001 [WNS001], WNSAMP, and
  WNS74A) are used as source radioactivity discharged from the WVDP during the year.

  The liquid effluents from those discharge points travel to Erdman Brook and Franks Creek on site, then leave the WVDP
  premises and flow into Buttermilk Creek east of the site. Buttermilk Creek merges into Cattaraugus Creek north of the
  WVDP, and then Cattaraugus Creek discharges into Lake Erie. Felton Bridge on Cattaraugus Creek is approximately three
  miles (4.8 km) from the WVDP; the Main Bridge in Gowanda on Cattaraugus Creek is approximately 12 miles (19 km),
  Lake Erie is about 28 miles (45 km) downstream. For dose calculations, dilution rates of three isotopes (strontium-90,
  cesium-137, and tritium) between the discharge points and the modeling points are incorporated into the GENII models.

  At the WVDP, GENII version 1 was used for waterborne dose modeling through 2023. In 2022, the population for the col-
  lective dose calculation was updated to match the population used in the airborne dose calculation model. In 2024, GENII
  version was upgraded to v2 and input values for both individual and collective dose models were updated to reflect the
  latest available data. Additionally, GENII’s internal conversion factors were updated to match DOE DCS 2022 water
  ingestion effective dose coefficients. Based on these changes, the new waterborne UDFs were finalized in 2025.

Dose From Air and Water Pathways
The total estimated potential dose from the WVDP in 2025 is a combination of a dose from airborne exposure based on mea-
surements from the AAMN and a modeled dose from water exposure.

Table 5-2 summarizes the annual total dose from both the air and water exposure pathways. The estimated annual
individual dose to the public from both airborne and liquid effluents released from the WVDP activities in 2025 was
0.009 mrem (0.00009 mSv). This annual total dose is entirely from the waterborne MEOSI dose because the ambi-
ent airborne dose was zero. The annual total collective dose was 0.1 person-rem; the same as the waterborne dose.
The table compares the annual doses to the EPA and DOE annual dose limits as well as the annual natural background radiation.
The 2025 results demonstrate the minuscule doses attributed to the WVDP activities in the year.

Figure 5-5 on page 5-11 illustrates the estimated doses from the airborne and waterborne pathways over the past 10 years.
The estimated airborne dose was significantly reduced after the WVDP began estimating airborne dose based on actual
positive detections from the AAMN sample filters, which seldom occurred. Prior to 2022, the airborne dose was estimated
based on laboratory analytical uncertainties due to the absence of positive detections. For the details of the ambient air
monitoring at the WVDP, please refer to the inset, “Airborne Dose Estimates Using CAP88-PC at the WVDP,” on page 5-7. The
estimated water-borne dose has decreased due to the reduction of strontium-90 concentrations in the WNSWAMP after the
PTW installation in 2010, as well as the water management update, which reduced the volume of industrial water flowing
into lagoon 3 (and discharged from outfall 001).

Dose from Food Samples
The dose from local food consumption is estimated based on actual food samples collected near the WVDP. Vegetables, fruit,
milk, venison, and fish samples from the WVDP vicinity are collected and analyzed for radiological constituents. (Biological
sampling locations are shown on Figures A-11 and A-14.) As stated in Chapter 4, “Environmental Monitoring,” milk and
venison (deer meat) samples are collected every year and fish, apples, beans, and corn are collected every five years. The five-
year interval samples were last sampled in 2022. Between the five-year sampling years, the WVDP uses the last five-year sam-
pling results in dose calculation. The DOE has developed Ingestion Dose Conversion Factors (DCFs) for DOE sites to convert mea-
sured radioactivity concentrations in food into dose. The ingested radioactivity in food multiplied by these DCFs provides the
estimated maximum potential dose for the food-only pathway. (Note: The estimated dose from the food pathway for 2025 uses
the DCFs from DOE/EH-0071. The DCFs for the radioisotopes used in the food dose provided in DOE/EH-0071 are very similar
to, and more conservative than, the more recent DCFs for these isotopes for ingested water and milk in DOE-STD-1196-2022.)

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 5-13

2025 Estimated Dose from Food

The estimated ingestion dose from food items, including 2025 samples of milk and venison and 2022 samples of fish, fruit, and
crops, was 0.43 mrem/year for an individual residing in the regions of the WVDP. This is approximately 0.1% of the average nat-
ural background radiation a U.S. resident receives annually (310 mrem/year). Strontium-90 was detected in one of the near-site
venison samples and cesium-137 was detected in one of the control (background) venison samples. Tritium was detected in
one of the venison background samples. Currently, there is no single limit for tritium concentration in food in the U.S., however,
the detected concentration (9.53E-08 µCi/mL) is five orders of magnitude lower than the DOE’s water ingestion standard for
tritium (2.6E-03 µCi/mL). Strontium-90 and cesium-137 from nuclear bomb testing and major nuclear accidents exist in natural
soils and are detected in wild animal tissues at very low levels. Potassium-40 was detected in the milk and venison samples as
in previous years. Potassium-40 is a naturally occurring radioisotope that is frequently detected in food samples. Food
sampling results in 2025 are tabulated in Tables E-1 and E-2. All concentration levels of positive results in the 2025 food
samples were statistically indistinguishable from those in the background samples (Table 4-2).

Dose to Biota
DOE Order 458.1 requires protection of the local biota from potential adverse effects from radioactivity released from DOE site
operations and has established dose rate limits to assist in evaluating the effects. The WVDP uses RESRAD-BIOTA v 1.8 (April
2016), the DOE-approved biota dose assessment software, to compute and assess the annual doses from the WVDP activities to
the target area (the WNYNSC premises). A description of the biota dose standard and dose calculation and assessment methods
are provided in the inset box, “Biota Dose Modeling Methodology using RESRAD-BIOTA” on page 5-16.

2025 Biota Dose Modeling Results
The RESRAD-BIOTA model was run using WVDP site-specific input concentrations of surface water, soil, and sediment to assess
radiation exposure to biota on the WNYNSC premises. For 2025, the surface water data measured in 2025 and soil and sediment
data up to 2022 were used in the model. (Soil and sediment samples are collected every five years.)

Following the graded approach described in DOE-STD-1153-2019, the maximum potential biota dose was first modeled as
site-specific screening using the maximum of measured radionuclide concentrations from surface waters, sediments, and soils.
The resulting dose exceeded applicable limits for both aquatic and terrestrial evaluations in 2025. Then, the biota dose model
was run again as site-specific analysis using average radionuclide concentrations derived from measurements from the same
medium used for the maximum biota dose assessment. Average concentrations more closely represent actual environmental
conditions. The resulting doses using average concentrations were below the annual dose limits set in DOE Order 458.1 and are
summarized in Table 5-4. The measurements of 12 radioisotopes are input into the RESRAD-BIOTA model. However, only the
results for cesium-137 and strontium-90 are shown in Table 5-4 because these two isotopes contribute the largest components
of both aquatic and terrestrial doses to biota, based on air and water monitoring data at the WVDP.

Table 5-4 shows that the sums of fractions for the aquatic and terrestrial evaluations were 2.51E-01 and 5.72E-01, respec-
tively, compared with the BCGs, with the higher dose occurring in the terrestrial system. The sum of fractions for both the
aquatic and terrestrial evaluations was less than 1.0, indicating that applicable Biota Concentration Guides (BCGs) were not
exceeded, therefore, populations of aquatic and terrestrial biota (both plants and animals) on the WNYNSC premises are not
being exposed to doses above DOE standards.

The populations of organisms most sensitive (most likely adversely affected) to strontium-90 and cesium-137 via the aquatic
and terrestrial pathways were riparian animals (such as the raccoon [aquatic dose]) and terrestrial animals (such as the wood-
chuck [terrestrial dose]).

Release of Materials Containing Residual Radioactivity

In addition to discharges to the environment, the release of property containing residual radioactive materials is considered a
potential contributor to the dose received by the public, as set forth in DOE Order 458.1.

The DOE sites are authorized to release and clear scrap metals, including materials possibly contaminated with radioactivity.
However, the release procedure has to adhere to the requirements outlined in DOE Order 458.1, “Radiation Protection of
the Public and the Environment.” Programs to ensure the safe release of materials from the DOE sites have been established,
including radiological assessment and survey procedures. A release of scrap metals is permitted only when it is assured that the
release does not incur adverse effects on either the public or the environment. No potentially radiologically contaminated scrap
metal was released from the WVDP in 2025.

Risk Assessment
Very high doses of radiation are known to cause adverse health effects, including cancer in humans and other organisms. For
example, 50 rem (50,000 mrem) of radiation exposure causes damage to blood cells. 50,000 mrem is about five million times
the radiation a patient is exposed to for a chest x-ray (0.01 rem) (The U.S. CDC [U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention],
2024). Over the past several decades, the radiation health physics community has conducted considerable research into the
biological effects of a prolonged exposure to low-dose radiation to develop risk estimates for cancer and other health effects.
‘low-dose radiation’ refers to radiation levels of near zero to 10 rem (10,000 mrem) or dose rates of less than 500 mrem per
hour (National Academies of Science, Engineering, and Medicine, 2022).

The Biological Effects of Ionizing Radiation (BEIR) VII Phase 2 report, “Health Risks from Exposure to Low Levels of Ionizing
Radiation” (NRC, 2006) estimated that, assuming a sex and age distribution similar to that of the entire U.S. population, on
average, approximately 1 person in 100 would be expected to develop cancer from a radiation dose of 10,000 mrem (100 mSv),
while approximately 42 of the 100 individuals would be expected to develop cancer from all other causes. The Interagency

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 5-15

 Biota Dose Modeling Methodology Using RESRAD-BIOTA
  The DOE has prepared a technical standard that provides methods and guidance to be used to evaluate doses from ionizing
  radiation to populations of aquatic animals, riparian animals, terrestrial plants, and terrestrial animals. The technical stan-
  dard, “A Graded Approach for Evaluating Radiation Doses to Aquatic and Terrestrial Biota” (DOE-STD-1153-2019, 2002),
  describes the dose evaluation method and had been referenced to evaluate radiation doses to aquatic and terrestrial biota
  within the premises of the WNYNSC, which includes the WVDP.

  RESRAD-BIOTA (version 1.8, April 2016), a calculation tool provided by the DOE for implementing the technical standard,
  takes in radioisotope concentration levels in environmental media (surface water, soil, and sediment) in the target area
  and estimates doses to the biota based on those concentrations, and compares the concentrations (pCi/L) to the Biota
  Concentration Guides (BCGs) (pCi/L), the static concentration standards for each isotope in each media that leads to the
  BIOTA dose limits.

  Following the STD-1153-2019 guidance, RESRAD-BIOTA enables a graded approach in a biota dose assessment. The maxi-
  mum concentrations (for site-specific screening) and average concentrations (for site-specific analysis) data from the target
  area are input into RESRAD-BIOTA. At the WVDP, surface water data obtained from the current year and historical soil/
  sediment data are input into the model. Surface water is sampled annually with the frequencies determined by the WVDP
  sampling program for each location. The soil and sediment sampling frequency was changed from annually to every five
  years in 2007. Therefore, for 2025, the most recent sediment samples included those collected from 2005–2007, 2012,
  2017 and 2022 and the most recent routine on-site surface soil sampling collected from 1995–2007, 2012, 2017 and 2022.
  Historical on-site surface soil sampling data from several special projects were also used. A longer sampling interval is
  used for soil and sediment because radionuclide concentrations change more rapidly over time in surface waters than in
  sediments and soils.

  Calculated concentrations in each level of screening are assessed against the BCGs, which lead to the DOE standard (DOE
  Order 458.1) presented in DOE-STD-1153-2019, Table 1-1, for compliance:

 <1.0 rad/d for aquatic animals;
 <0.1 rad/d for riparian animals;
 <1.0 rad/d for terrestrial plants; and
 <0.1 rad/d for terrestrial animals.

  (Note that the absorbed dose unit [rad] is used for biota instead of the units used for indicating human risk [rem]).

  The concentration of each radionuclide in each medium is divided by its BCG for the particular biota category to calculate a
  fraction. The fractions of all radioisotope concentrations for each biota category are added to produce a sum of fractions. If
  the sum of fractions is less than 1.0, the exposure to the particular biota category is less than the corresponding dose limit.
  The biota category, which has a higher sum of fractions, is considered as a limiting species.

Steering Committee on Radiation Standards of the EPA (ISCORS, 2002) and DOE guidance estimate that the probability of fatal
cancer occurring from exposure to radioactivity is between one and six cancer cases per 10,000 people who are each exposed
to one rem (1,000 mrem [10 mSv]) (i.e., a risk coefficient of between 0.0001 and 0.0006). These assumptions are often based
on a cancer risk model known as the linear no-threshold (LNT) model.

Discussions about and a validation study of the LNT model are ongoing (Trabalka et al., 2017). Some scientists argue that the
cancer risk is not linearly correlated to exposure to low levels of radiation, citing human cells’ natural ability to repair minor
damage, or the beneficial effect, hormesis, on organisms of low-dose radiation (Agathokleous, 2024). Some research suggests
that researchers should consider various cancer triggers (or causes), including historical data, to estimate cancer risk from radi-
ation exposure (Bencko, 2011; Kellerer, 2000).

5-16 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

 Wild Fauna and Flora We Protect in the Nearby Environment and at the Site

 (photo credits: top right - R. McCloud; top left/bottom left - M. Regan; bottom right: F. Cohen)

The radiological effluents from the WVDP are very low in radioactivity in all media. The predicted dose from all pathways in
2025 from the WVDP activities was 0.009 mrem or 0.00009 mSv. Employing the ISCORS’ probability of fatal cancer from radia-
tion of six persons per 10,000 people per one rem, a cancer risk from the WVDP activities in 2025 was 0.54 cancer cases per
100 million people. Figure 5-1, earlier in this chapter, provides a more generic perspective on the small level of the emissions
from the WVDP activities.

Dose Assessment Summary
The 2025 total estimated dose (0.009 mrem [0.00009 mSv]) from the WVDP activities to a hypothetical MEOSI, including air-
borne and waterborne doses, was 0.009% of the federal standard of 100 mrem for dose from all pathways allowed from any
DOE site operations in a calendar year. The annual population dose of 2025 (0.1 person-rem) was 0.1% of the DOE’s collective
dose guideline, 100 person-rem. Radioactivity concentrations in milk and venison samples were not distinguishable from those
of the background. Biota dose estimates indicate that populations of plants and animals at the WVDP are only exposed to a
fraction of the DOE standards for dose to biota. These results confirm that efforts at the WVDP to minimize radiological releases
from all effluent pathways worked efficiently in 2025.

Based on the overall dose assessment, the WVDP was found to be in compliance with applicable effluent radiological guidelines
and standards during 2025.

5-18 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

 CHAPTER 6
 GROUNDWATER PROTECTION PROGRAM
WVDP Groundwater Monitoring Program Overview
The WVDP groundwater monitoring program (WVDP GMP) consists of three areas of focus, based on compliance requirements,
specific physical locations, and monitoring purposes.

 1. Groundwater Monitoring Program (GMP) – Site-wide monitoring/environmental surveillance per DOE Orders and RCRA
 §3008(h) Compliance using wells located across the north and south plateaus.

 2. Permeable Treatment Wall Performance Monitoring Program (PTWPMP) – Monitoring of the PTW using dedicated wells,
 which are located adjacent to and within the PTW.

 3. North Plateau Groundwater Monitoring Program (NPGMP) – Supplements the PTWPMP using additional wells located
 further upgradient and downgradient of the PTW.

This chapter outlines the program details and presents the monitoring results for calendar year 2025. Monitoring results for the
PTWPMP and NPGMP are discussed together.

Geology and Hydrogeology at the WVDP
The WNYNSC is situated upon a layered sequence of glacial sediments that fill a steep-sided bedrock valley composed of
interbedded shales and siltstones (Rickard, 1975). The glacial sediments overlying the bedrock consist of three silt- and clay-rich
glacial tills of Lavery, Kent, and possibly Olean deposits, which are separated by stratified fluvio-lacustrine deposits (silty or silty/
sandy lakebed sediments). (See Table 6-1 for a detailed description of each geologic layer.)

Listed in progression from bottom to top, the bedrock beneath the WVDP is overlain by the Kent till (KT), followed by the Kent
Recessional Sequence (KRS) (layers of clay or silty clay topped by coarser sands and gravels), and the unweathered Lavery Till
(ULT). See Figure 6-1 and Table 6-1 for the geographic distribution and additional description of these units.

Erdman Brook traverses the WVDP site, dividing it into a north and south plateau. The uppermost surficial sediments covering
the north and south plateaus are quite different. On the south plateau, the ULT is exposed at the surface, where it is weathered
and fractured, and is referred to as the weathered Lavery Till (WLT). On the north plateau, the ULT is covered by an alluvial sand
and gravel (S&G) unit, composed of silty sands and gravels. It can be further divided into two subunits: the thick-bedded unit
(TBU), a thick bed of silty sands and gravels, and the slackwater sequence (SWS), composed of thin layered silts and fine sands.
Within the ULT on the north plateau is a smaller unit called the Lavery Till sand (LTS), an isolated sandy unit entirely contained
within the till (WVNSCO, January 1993; WVNSCO, February 1993).

The MPPB footprint, WTF, and lagoons are located on the north plateau. The drum cell, NDA, and SDA are located on the south
plateau. The ULT and Kent till have relatively low permeability, and groundwater from the S&G and WLT must flow through
the ULT to reach the KRS and deeper underlying geologic units. Therefore, the ULT, KRS, Kent till, and bedrock do not provide
predominant pathways for contaminant movement from the WVDP and are not discussed here. The S&G unit and the WLT are
generally regarded as the predominant routes for contaminant migration from the WVDP via groundwater.

The majority of groundwater migrating across the site discharges from the S&G and WLT into the surrounding streams, which
eventually flow into Cattaraugus Creek and then into Lake Erie. Past surveys determined that no community public water sup-
plies are drawn from the groundwater-bearing zones downgradient of the site or from Cattaraugus Creek downstream of the
WVDP. Upgradient of the site, groundwater is used as a private drinking water supply by local residents.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 6-1

Site Groundwater Use
Since 2014, two bedrock water supply wells, WNDWELL 1 and WNDWELL 2, have been used to provide the site’s primary water
supply. These two wells are located upgradient of the site facilities and areas of possible contamination (Figure A-8). Chemical
and radiological sampling is performed routinely, and sampling results for drinking water quality parameters are provided to the
Cattaraugus County Health Department (CCHD). Site groundwater in the shallow, unconsolidated glacial sediments located
above the bedrock is not used for drinking or operational purposes, nor is WVDP effluent discharged directly to groundwater.
Drinking water data from the water supply wells are presented in Appendix B-4.

Groundwater Monitoring Program (GMP)
The WVDP GMP was designed to comply with DOE Order 458.1, Section 4.i.2, which states that “Groundwater must be protect-
ed from radiological contamination to ensure compliance with dose limits in the Order. DOE sites must ensure that: baseline
conditions of the groundwater quantity and quality are documented; possible sources of, and potential for, radiological con-
tamination are identified and assessed; strategies to control radiological contamination are documented and implemented;
monitoring methodologies are documented and implemented; and groundwater monitoring activities are integrated with other
environmental monitoring activities.”

The GMP also complies with the requirements of the RCRA §3008(h) Administrative Order on Consent and the conclusions in
the RCRA Facility Investigation (RFI) reports, which require routine monitoring of certain analytes at specified groundwater
monitoring locations.

Groundwater conditions are monitored in the subsurface geologic units that represent potential routes of contaminant migra-
tion. The monitoring analytes and frequency are tailored for each well, depending upon location, nearby historical site opera-
tions, and the potential for contaminant migration.

Results are evaluated to identify current conditions and historical trends, and final conclusions are reported to both state and fed-
eral agencies in addition to the discussions in this report. Methods for evaluating groundwater data are described in Appendix D.
Highlights of the site groundwater monitoring history and the evolution of the GMP are summarized at the end of this chapter
in Table 6-5.

Groundwater Monitoring Network
Groundwater is monitored across the north and south plateaus using wells installed in the first six of the seven geologic units
described in Table 6-1. In 2025, GMP samples were collected from 65 of the 66 on-site wells (samples were not collected at
one well due to being dry) routine groundwater monitoring locations, of which,

  • 38 are RCRA monitoring wells and 27 are non-RCRA monitoring wells (see Table 6-4);

  • five are located at groundwater seepage points along the northeast edge of the north plateau, where groundwater from
 the S&G unit discharges to the ground surface along the banks of Franks Creek; and

  • one trench sump (NDATR) located within the interceptor trench installed at the NDA.

Well locations are displayed on Figures A-9 and A-10 in Appendix A. Many of the wells are used for monitoring releases from
one or more of the SWMUs or Super SWMUs (SSWMUs) on site (Table 6-2) per the RCRA §3008(h) Consent Order and the con-
clusions in the RFI reports. For RCRA discussions, refer to Chapter 2, “Environmental Compliance Summary” and Table A-2 for
the groundwater sampling schedule. No new GMP wells were installed or decommissioned during 2025. 2025 sampling results
at the 65 wells are shown in Appendix D-2 tables.

Groundwater Elevation Monitoring
Groundwater elevations are measured in the monitoring network wells in conjunction with the quarterly analytical sam-
pling. These data are used to map groundwater flow directions. Long-term trend graphs are used to evaluate variations in

6-2 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

groundwater elevations over time, which may result from cyclical seasonal fluctuations or on-site operations such as installing
water diversions (e.g., geomembrane covers, trenches, or slurry walls), and the groundwater treatment systems (e.g., the full-
scale PTW). Groundwater elevations on the north plateau of the current year are displayed in Figure 6-2. Although groundwater
elevations on the south plateau are not displayed in this report, the elevations are mapped quarterly and have helped evaluate
the effectiveness of the NDA interceptor trench, the slurry wall, and geomembrane cover.

North Plateau Strontium-90 Plume Monitoring
The groundwater well sampling network on the north plateau monitors potential and existing sources of chemical and radio-
logical contaminants. This includes areas of previously detected contamination such as the Construction and Demolition Debris
Landfill (CDDL) and the former lagoon 1 area. The current focus of radiological groundwater monitoring on the north plateau
is the gross beta groundwater plume.

Elevated gross beta activity has been detected in groundwater from the S&G unit, the shallowest geologic unit on the north
plateau, since 1993. An acid recovery line that leaked in the southwest corner of the MPPB during the late 1960s is considered
to be the source of this plume. The status of the plume is monitored with quarterly sampling at 28 monitoring wells and five
seepage monitoring locations on the north plateau S&G unit. Historical monitoring of groundwater has established that stron-
tium-90 is the predominant beta emitter found in the groundwater plume. Strontium-90 accounts for approximately one-half of
the gross beta activity because the gross beta activity includes both strontium-90 and its daughter product, yttrium-90.

For the discussions about gross beta and strontium-90 in this report, the strontium-90 DCS is used as a comparison level for
both gross beta and strontium-90 concentrations. Refer to the inset, “Radiological Data Evaluation” in Chapter 4, "Environmen-
tal Monitoring," for a discussion of the DCSs, and Table 8-4 for a list of the DCSs for radionuclides of interest at the WVDP.

GMP Radiological Monitoring Results in 2025
Several groundwater monitoring wells in the north plateau S&G unit were analyzed for a select list of radionuclides (including
strontium-90) in 2025, in addition to gross alpha, gross beta, and tritium. (See Table A-2 for sampling program details.)

Extent of Groundwater Plume

Figure 6-2 on page 6-6 illustrates the historical extent of the groundwater plume in the S&G unit, as defined by the
1.0E-06 µCi/mL gross beta isopleth, at three time intervals (1994, 2010, and 2025). Both the NYSDEC Technical and Operational
Guidance Series (TOGS).1.1.1 Class GA Groundwater Quality Standard, and the former DOE Derived Concentration Guide (DCG)
for strontium-90 are set to 1.0E-06 µCi/mL. The WVDP Groundwater Screening Level (GSL) for strontium-90 is also based on the
NYSDEC TOGS Standard (Table D-1A). Appendix D-1 explains the definition of GSL and the method by which a site-specific GSL
was established for each isotope. The 1994 isopleth shows the results of the subsurface characterization program that mea-
sured gross beta concentrations to define the outline of the plume discovered in 1993. The plume originated from beneath the
MPPB. (See the “1994” row in Table 6-5). The 2010 isopleth shows the furthest extent of the plume’s northeastward migration
before the installation of the PTW in 2010. The 2025 isopleth shows the most current extent of the plume at the 1.0E-06 µCi/mL
level. See “Permeable Treatment Wall Performance Monitoring Program (PTWPMP)” section later in this chapter for the details
of the PTW installation and 2025 results.

In Figure 6-2, flow arrows show groundwater flow directions across the north plateau. Groundwater generally flows northeast
across most of the north plateau; however, in the area directly south and east upgradient of the central portion of the PTW, the
PTW-area groundwater flows eastward. Some of the groundwater entering the PTW flows eastward within the PTW, diverting
upgradient groundwater to the east. This eastward flow has facilitated the migration of the groundwater plume to the eastern
edge of the plateau.

As shown in Figure 6-2, while the plume’s western boundary has not changed significantly since 1994, the plume has continued
to migrate to the northeast and east across the north plateau, extending its spatial extent toward those directions. The leading
edge of the plume is divided into three narrow lobes as a result of the uneven distribution of coarse and fine soils within the
S&G unit, which creates preferential pathways for groundwater flow.

(continued from page 6-3)

The portion of the plume downgradient of the PTW is the result of residual gross beta activity that had previously migrated past the
location of the PTW prior to its installation. The three lobes downgradient of the PTW have elongated since 2010 as the residual
strontium-90 continues its northeastward transport across the north plateau. The western lobe of the plume flows northward to-
wards and into the swamp ditch, which conducts water to the northeast towards the perimeter of the north plateau. The central
lobe of the plume also flows towards the swamp ditch’s northeast end, merging with the northward flow. (See "Northeast Swamp
Drainage Monitoring" section on page 6-19.) These lobes continue to attenuate as treated groundwater exiting the PTW flushes
the legacy plumes from the groundwater system. The PTW monitoring program indicates localized segments show elevated
strontium-90 or gross beta concentrations within the wall and slightly downgradient, which is expected over its lifecycle.

Gross beta concentration trends over the last 10 years at selected monitoring wells located within the plume are shown on
Figures 6-4 through Figures 6-8. These data are plotted on a log scale; therefore, an increase from one grid line to the next

6-8 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

represents a 10-fold increase in concentration. The log scale was used to enable comparison between data from background
locations (with concentrations in the 1.0E-09 µCi/mL range) and data from the central plume (with concentrations in the
1.0E-04 µCi/mL range, 100,000 times higher than the background).

Figure 6-3 shows the north plateau groundwater plume as of December 2025. Each colored area represents a 10-fold increase
in gross beta concentration, ranging from 1.0E-08 to 1.0E-04 µCi/mL. The highest concentration range (≥1.0E-04 µCi/mL) is
the area that originates beneath the former MPPB and extends to the northeast towards the PTW, where it begins to divide
into narrow lobes, presumably flowing along preferential pathways within the subsurface sediments. The next highest range
includes concentrations between 1.0E-4 and 1.0E-05 µCi/mL; this range extends from the source area up to the PTW, where its
further migration is mostly halted by being treated within the zeolite of the PTW.

As mentioned earlier, some gross beta activity intermittently is able to migrate through the PTW and exit from the downgradi-
ent side in areas where the zeolite is becoming saturated with strontium-90 and other positively charged ions.

2025 Monitoring Results Upgradient of the PTW

Figures 6-4 through 6-6 illustrate the annual average gross beta concentrations in wells monitoring the groundwater plume in
the main portion of the north plateau, from the source area up to the PTW. The background concentrations (orange lines) on
these graphs represent the averages of monitoring results at wells 301, 401, 706, and 1302, which are located upgradient (on
the west side) of the MPPB.

Figure 6-4 illustrates the annual average gross beta concentrations in groundwater wells located immediately downgradient of
the MPPB and along the western edge of the plume (at well 8609). Well 408 and the four MPPB wells (MP-01, -02, -03, and -04),
located northeast of the MPPB closest to the source area, historically exhibit the highest gross beta concentrations of any rou-
tinely monitored wells in the GMP. MP-01 and -02 were not sampled in 2023 and 2024 during the MPPB deconstruction work
due to their proximity to the deconstruction zone. The gross beta concentrations at these wells increased from 2019 through
2021. An investigation found that a 100% sodium chloride (100% salt) deicing material applied during the 2019-2020 and 2020-
2021 winters was impacting the geochemistry of the groundwater and was the cause of the increase. The deicing material was
changed back to 3:1 sand/salt during the 2021-2022 winter. Strontium-90 levels in those wells have not exceeded the 2020-
2021 levels since then. Between 2024 and 2025, gross beta at wells MP-03, MP-04 and 8609 slightly increased, however, the
concentrations are within the historical range. The average gross beta at the background wells has increased since 2024, and

tritium, were found at wells MP-01, MP-03, and MP-04. Strontium-90 increases at wells 301, 401 (both west of the MPPB) and
406 (north of the MPPB) will be closely monitored in 2026.

central lobe of the plume where gross beta activity had migrated past the PTW before it was installed in 2010 (Figure 6-2). Well

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 6-11

8612 is located in the area where the swamp ditch and the central plume lobe intersect (Figure 6-3). However, the steep con-
centration increase at 8612 suggests that residual strontium-90 migration from the plume was affected by the sodium chloride
influx to the groundwater in 2019 through 2021, which increased strontium-90 transport throughout the groundwater system.
These downgradient monitoring points still reflect the attenuation of the sodium chloride in the system. The strontium-90 is
expected to decline as seen in upgradient wells where the sodium chloride already attenuated.

The concentrations at well 804 on the east side of CDDL have decreased, suggesting that treated groundwater exiting the PTW
near this well is attenuating the residual gross beta activity in this area. Historical strontium-90 concentration by year at the
natural discharge points WNSWAMP and WNSW74A are presented in Figure 4-14 on page 4-14.

Monitoring results of gross beta concentrations at seepage points along the northeastern edge of the plateau demonstrated
mixed trends since 2021 (Figure 6-8). Seepage point SP-12, which has shown a constant increasing trend since 2021, is located
at the northeastern edge of the 1E-06 µCi/mL gross beta isopleth (Figure 6-2). The gross beta concentrations at SP-12 exceeded
the strontium-90 DCS of 1.7E-06 µCi/mL in 2023 and increased through 2025. The gross beta concentrations at GSEEP on the
eastern side of the seepage area increased and exceeded the concentration of 1E-06 µCi/mL in 2025. The seepage monitoring
point SP-06 (north of GSEEP) and SP-11 (south of SP-12) demonstrated moderate gross beta concentration increases in 2025,
while SP-04 (between SP-06 and GSEEP) showed a decrease in the concentrations.

These gross beta concentration increases in the last few years in this area are caused by several factors, including the continued
migration of the residual gross beta activity present downgradient of the PTW; the mobilization of strontium-90 from the sub-
surface by the elevated sodium and chloride concentrations that migrated across the north plateau; and strontium-90 exiting
the PTW in areas impacted by the sodium and chloride or from areas where the zeolite is becoming expended (strontium-90
exchange rate is declining). An investigation to evaluate options for improving the PTW's effectiveness and increasing its longev-
ity was completed in 2025, and potential further actions are being evaluated. (For further discussion of the PTW performance
evaluation, refer to “Permeable Treatment Wall Performance Monitoring Program (PTWPMP)” on page 6-16. ) At well 801,
immediately downgradient of the PTW, the figure suggests that gross beta concentrations continued decreasing since 2021.

Tritium in North Plateau Groundwater
 former
 lagoon 1
Tritium, a fission product of the nuclear power fuel cycle, has historically been
detected on the north plateau. Elevated tritium concentrations have been ob-
served downgradient of the MPPB and adjacent to and downgradient of the lagoon system. The tritium activities are attributed
to residual tritium in the nuclear fuel that was reprocessed on the site by Nuclear Fuel Services, Inc. (NFS).

Table 6-3 shows the maximum concentration (µCi/mL) of radioisotopes and the well IDs where the maximum values were ob-
served. In Table 6-3, the maximum concentrations are compared against the site-specific GSLs (screening levels). The maximum
tritium concentration measured in groundwater from the north plateau in 2025 was 5.22E-07 µCi/mL at well MP-01, located on
the east side of the MPPB and northeast of the former lagoon 1. (See Figure 6-2 for the well location). The concentration is about
2.0E-07 µCi/mL higher than the one in 2024, however, was still four orders of magnitude below the DOE DCS. In general, the
maximum tritium concentrations among wells have decreased over the years.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 6-13

Radiological Groundwater Monitoring Downgradient of the Waste Tank Farm (WTF)

Liquid radioactive waste remaining in the underground HLW tanks after the completion of the vitrification process is stabilized
by the T&VDS, which reduces the liquid volume in the tanks and vaults through evaporation. Three of the tanks have become
dry, and the liquid levels in the fourth tank are decreasing. (See Chapter 2, “Environmental Compliance Summary” for additional
information.)

Groundwater profile downgradient of the WTF has been controlled by periodic pumping of a dewatering well located adjacent
to the tank vaults. Pumping of the well maintains a low groundwater level outside the vaults to reduce the upward hydrostatic
pressure on the vaults and minimize the potential for groundwater infiltration into the vaults. Although the pumped water as
well as the water collected in low areas around the WTF is radiologically clean, the water is transferred to the interceptor system
and managed as potentially contaminated water as a conservative precaution.

At groundwater monitoring well 8607 north of the WTF and at wells 406 and 409 northeast of the WTF, positive gross beta
concentrations were observed in 2025. (See the location of well 8607 on Figure 6-3.) However, the concentrations were four to
five orders of magnitude lower than at well 408 located in the gross beta plume area (Figure 6-4).

GMP Non-Radiological Monitoring Results in 2025
2025 Results for Volatile and Semivolatile Organic Compounds (VOCs and SVOCs)

Select wells within the S&G unit are monitored for VOCs and SVOCs per the 3008(h) Consent Order. The concentrations of
these compounds were detected at levels exceeding the Series (TOGS) 1.1.1 Class GA Groundwater Quality Standards in some
groundwater samples collected during the RFI. The only S&G unit monitoring location with previously consistent positive VOC
detections was well 8612 located northeast and downgradient of the CDDL. Figure 6-10 provides a summary of the concentra-
tion ranges of four VOCs that have been historically detected at well 8612. None of these VOCs have been detected above the
Practical Quantitation Limit (PQL) since 2015. The presumed source of the VOCs previously detected in well 8612 is from wastes
buried in the CDDL. Some of these VOCs, such as dichlorodifluoromethane (DCDFMeth) and 1,1-dichloroethane (1,1-DCA) have
been frequently detected at estimated concentrations below the PQL of 5 ug/L.

Tributyl phosphate (TBP), an SVOC, has been detected in groundwater from well 8605 (Figure 6-3), located downgradient of the
former lagoon 1 and solvent dike. (See Figure 6-11 on the next page.) The presence of TBP is thought to be caused by residual
contamination from liquid waste management activities in the former lagoon 1 area during nuclear fuel reprocessing. The con-
centration levels have steadily decreased and have stayed below about 50.0 micrograms per liter [µg/L] since 2017. The max-
imum TBP concentration measured in 2025 at well 8605 was 18.0 µg/L which exceeds the PQL of 10 µg/L. A TOGS 1.1.1 water
quality standard has not been established for TBP. Besides TBP, three volatile organic compounds were detected at estimated
amounts below the PQL of 5 µg/L at well 803 (north side of the PTW).

The EPA’s emerging contaminants of concern currently include organic chemicals such as 1,4-dioxane and 1,2,3-trichloropro-
pane (TCP), as well as PFAS substances. On April 10, 2024, the EPA announced the final National Primary Drinking Water Regula-
tion (NPDWR) for six PFAS substances and PFAS mixtures. 1,4-dioxane was not detected in the samples collected from the site’s
groundwater monitoring wells in 2025. Samples collected from the WVDP drinking water supply well for the analysis of PFAS
substances were also nondetect (Appendix B-4).

2025 Results for Metals Sampling

The naturally occurring metal concentrations in the site groundwater were determined using data collected from background
wells 301, 401, 706, and 1302 (from 1991 to 2008), which are located upgradient of the plume source area. The results were
used to calculate background groundwater concentrations for the metals listed in 6 NYCRR 373-2 Appendix 33 which were set
to the mean concentrations plus two standard deviations.

The background concentrations were also examined against the NYSDEC TOGS 1.1.1 Class A Groundwater Quality Standards.
It was determined that the metals detected were not associated with the groundwater plume. Well 706 has continued to be
sampled for metals primarily because of the elevated chromium and nickel concentrations. (See Table D-1B for the standards.)

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 6-15

 As preparation for the Phase 1B below-grade removal and soil remediation, core sampling to
 characterize radiological and geochemical components began in October, 2025

Routine metals sampling continued to be performed in 2025, as outlined in the GMP. The sampling results were compared with
the established GSLs and background levels. The only metals detected above the GSLs in groundwater in 2025 were chromium
and nickel at two locations, wells 405 and 706. Wells 405 and 706 are stainless steel wells that have historically shown evidence
of corrosion. Chromium and nickel can leach from the corroding well screen and become adsorbed to fine sediments within the
wells which are then entrained in the groundwater samples collected for analysis. Arsenic, just marginally above the GSL, was
detected at one location (well MP-02). The majority of sampling results for arsenic at well MP-02 have been nondetect, but it
has been detected intermittently. Only one of the two sampling results for arsenic at MP-02 exceeded the GSL in 2025. Barium
was detected at wells 909 and NDATR located at the corner of the NDA Interceptor Trench. The detected concentrations were
below the corresponding GSL. (See Appendix D-2F tables.)

Figure 6-12 on the next page shows the well locations where radiological and metal sampling results exceeded the GSLs in 2025.

Permeable Treatment Wall Performance Monitoring Program (PTWPMP)
History of the PTW and the PTW Structure

The PTW on the north plateau (Figure 6-13) is an underground wall with granular clinoptilolite (i.e., zeolite), a natural miner-
al with a porous structure. The zeolite traps positively charged ions, including strontium-90, by ion exchange while allowing
the groundwater to pass through. Prior to the full-scale PTW installation in 2010, a pilot-scale PTW was installed in 1999,
which helped determine that the planned PTW technology was an effective remediation method for treating strontium-90-
contaminated groundwater. Underground treatment walls are utilized at multiple nuclear sites, not only at the WVDP, to treat
contaminated groundwater plumes, such as at the Hanford Site where a liquid calcium-citrate-phosphate was injected into a
vertical treatment wall.

The full-scale PTW was constructed by excavating an 860-foot-long trench through the entire thickness of the S&G unit (in-
cluding the TBU and the SWS, where present), and keying into the underlying, low-permeability ULT. The excavation was then
filled with zeolite. A lined stormwater drainage ditch (Smart-Ditch™) was also installed in 2010, south of the PTW, to intercept
stormwater from upland site areas and route it around the PTW to Franks Creek. A map view of the PTW location, along with a
vertical cross-section showing the geology excavated for the PTW installation, is presented in Figure 6-13.

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 6-17

The design of the PTW addressed three remedial action objectives (RAOs): reduce or eliminate Sr-90 in seepage leaving the
north plateau; minimize the future expansion of the plume beyond its mapped limits at the time of the PTW installation; and
not preclude any strategies for future plume management during site decommissioning.

The PTW placement was chosen not to transect the CDDL (landfill) and to limit the expansion of groundwater impacted by
strontium-90. At the time of the PTW installation, the strontium-90 concentration in the plume approaching the PTW was at
about 1.0E-05 µCi/mL (10,000 pCi/L) level. One of the design requirements of the PTW was to limit the expansion of the plume
at or above this concentration level. The full-scale PTW, by design, did not capture the plume’s leading edge downgradient of
the PTW structure as it existed in November 2010. North plateau groundwater monitoring after the PTW installation has shown
that groundwater exiting the PTW has lower strontium-90 concentrations than groundwater entering the PTW installation, in
general, proving the effectiveness of the PTW treatment.

The DOE’s Phase 1B for decommissioning activities for the WVDP include excavating subsurface soils in the plume source area
below and surrounding area of WMA 1. Long-term strategies for managing the nonsource area of the plume, including the PTW,
will be evaluated as part of the Phase 2 decision-making process for the WVDP and the WNYNSC.

Updates for 2025 PTW Monitoring

PTW performance monitoring data collected during 2025, indicate the following:

  • Groundwater flow patterns in the PTW area are similar to flow patterns observed in previous years. As mentioned earlier,
 some groundwater flows parallel within the central portion of the PTW and causes some of the upgradient groundwater
 to be diverted to the east, entering the eastern section of the wall.

  • With the exception of one sample at one well, the PTW prevented the migration of strontium-90 activities above
 1.0E-05 µCi/mL from flowing past the wall during 2025 (Figure 6-3).

  • Geochemical differences (i.e., decreases in the concentrations of strontium-90 and certain other cations) observed in
 groundwater that has migrated into or through the zeolite indicate that ion exchange is occurring in the wall.

6-18 WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025

  • During the past few years, elevated strontium-90 activity has been detected in a few different zeolite wells. These mea-
 surements have indicated that some isolated areas of the PTW are not functioning at the levels they once did and are
 less effective at treating the strontium-90 plume. These degraded spots are thought to be associated with the elongation
 of the west and central gross beta lobes downgradient of the PTW. (See Figures 6-2 and 6-3.) It is also possible that the
 aforementioned sodium chloride deicing material accelerated strontium-90 exchange in and release from zeolite and gave
 an appearance that the PTW was becoming less effective. A study to identify methods to enhance the effectiveness and
 increase the longevity of the PTW was completed in 2024 and the options are being evaluated.

 Despite increases in strontium-90 activities in select locations, overall strontium-90 activity in groundwater immediately
 downgradient of the PTW remained lower than upgradient levels in 2025, which indicates the PTW is still operating as
 designed.

The NPGMP, which supplements the PTW monitoring program, monitors groundwater plume migration farther upgradient
and downgradient of the areas monitored by the PTWPMP. Under the NPGMP, quarterly gross beta sampling and water level
measurements supplement the PTWPMP well data for evaluating plume conditions. However, these data are not reported in
this ASER.

Northeast Swamp Drainage Monitoring
The western and central lobes of the plume downgradient of the PTW are partially intercepted by the northeast swamp drain-
age ditch flowing west to east across the plume’s leading edge. These waters ultimately flow into Franks Creek, then into
Buttermilk Creek north of the site (Figure 1-2). Strontium-90 concentrations at WNSWAMP have decreased since 2010, when
the PTW was installed. In the last three years, the concentrations have been below 1.00E-06 µCi/mL. Figure 4-4 in Chapter 4,
Environmental Monitoring,” shows the flow-weighted annual average strontium-90 concentration at WNSWAMP.

The seeps and the swamp drainage leave the site premises over time and enter surface water pathways (creeks). The releases
through WNSWAMP and WNSW74A, mostly consisting of strontium-90, accounted for an annual estimated waterborne dose of
0.004 mrem in 2025 to a hypothetical off-site individual on Cattaraugus Creek at Felton Bridge (sampling location WFFELBR), the
first downstream location accessible to the public. (See “2025 Potential Maximum Waterborne Dose to an Off-Site Individual”
in Chapter 5, “Dose Assessment.”)

South Plateau and NDA Groundwater Monitoring
Interim Measures (IMs)

In accordance with RCRA §3008(h) Administrative Order on Consent (page 2-9), an IM including a trench system, was construct-
ed in 1990 through the WLT along the northeast and northwest sides of the NDA to intercept and collect potentially contaminat-
ed groundwater. The NDA is located on the south plateau and is adjacent to the SDA, which is owned and managed by NYSERDA.
The sampling location NDATR (Figure A-10) is a sump located at the lowest point of the interceptor trench. Groundwater is
collected at NDATR and transferred to the Low Level Waste Treatment Facility (LLWTF) for processing.

 PTW Smart Ditch™ PTW monitoring wells

WVDP Annual Site Environmental Report - Calendar Year 2025 6-19

A second IM was completed in December 2008, aiming to improve the stability of the earthen cap and to limit the infiltration
of groundwater, surface water, and precipitation into the NDA. These measures included installing a geomembrane cap over
the NDA, a low-permeability upgradient slurry wall, and surface water drainage diversions. In 2019, the Liquid Pretreatment
System (LPS) in the northern portion of the NDA was deactivated and removed. A geomembrane patch was then applied over
the area and attached to the existing geomembrane cover.

In 2021, NYSERDA installed an additional section of barrier wall that was connected to their existing SDA barrier wall on the
upgradient side of SDA Trench 14 and northeast of the NDA. NYSERDA also extended the SDA geomembrane cover northwest
of SDA Trench 14 to further minimize water infiltration into the SDA (Figure 1-2). The SDA trenches and groundwater monitoring
results from the SDA wells are described in NYSERDA’s State-Licensed Disposal Area at West Valley Annual Reports.

2025 Update of NDA Interim Measure (IM) Monitoring

Groundwater elevations are monitored quarterly in and around the NDA and the interceptor trench to ensure that an inward
gradient is maintained. For the well locations at the NDA area, please refer to Figure A-10. Figure 6-14 shows the reduced water
volume collected from the interceptor trench since the geomembrane cap was installed in 2008. The removal of the LPS and
the patching of the geomembrane cap in 2019 further decreased water infiltration into the NDA. The total volume pumped
from the NDA trench in 2025 was 11,179 gal (42,317 L). The pumped water has been analyzed for gross alpha and gross
beta. The concentrations of gross beta fluctuated year by year, but overall, remained constant at about 4.00E-07 µCi/mL level.
Gross beta concentrations increased briefly when LPS was deactivated and removed, then rapidly decreased to the current
1.30E-07 µCi/mL level, below the strontium-90 DCS. Similar to the north plateau, strontium-90 is the main contributing radio-
isotope to the measured gross beta activity in the NDA trench water, which is attributable to the wastes buried in the NDA.

Water level data from piezometers installed to monitor the effects of the NDA IM indicate that the slurry wall and geomem-
brane cover continued to limit the inflow of precipitation and groundwater to the NDA in 2025, resulting in the WLT becoming
dry in some areas.
